# Chapter 1: Introduction to Virtual Coaching

### Welcome to NASM Virtual Coaching

Chapter 1Introduction to Virtual CoachingLesson 1Welcome to NASM Virtual CoachingWelcome to NASM Virtual CoachingThe NASM Virtual Coaching course is an immersive online experience that equips fitness professionals with the skills, tools, and strategies to operate a virtual coaching business. Throughout this course, professionals will learn how to foster an engaging and effective online relationship with their clients, leveraging various technologies. This course uses the virtual environment to provide the skills and knowledge to address topics including scope of practice, business planning, technology requirements, communication strategies, conducting virtual fitness assessments, implementing virtual coaching sessions, and fundamental business operations.

### Chapter 1: Introduction to Virtual Coaching

Chapter 1Introduction to Virtual CoachingLesson 1Welcome to NASM Virtual CoachingChapter 1: Introduction to Virtual CoachingVirtual coaching provides fitness professionals with the ability to train more clients, avoid geographical limitations, and increase revenue, but it comes with certain disadvantages. Without a physical training space and regular in-person sessions, fitness professionals often find it difficult differentiating themselves to attract new clients, developing or maintaining trust and communication, and monitoring compliance to avoid injuries and ensure outcomes. The NASM Virtual Coaching course provides fitness professionals with the necessary business, physical training, behavioral, and technological knowledge necessary to build a thriving virtual training business.
Learning ObjectivesAfter completing this chapter, the NASM Virtual Coach will have the ability to do the following:


    Define the role of a virtual coach.
    Identify potential opportunities from offering coaching services within a virtual environment.
    Determine the different client types who would benefit from virtual coaching services.
    Summarize the professional qualifications of a virtual coach.
    Explain professional scope of practice of a virtual coach.

---

### Introduction

Chapter 1Introduction to Virtual CoachingLesson 1Welcome to NASM Virtual CoachingIntroductionNot long ago, the fitness industry was centered around locations that included a built-in client base for fitness professionals: the gym. The current landscape of the fitness industry looks much different, with demand at an all-time high. According to the 2019 IHRSA Health Club Consumer Report, more than 71 million people in the United States reported being a member of a fitness center, gym, or studio in 2018 (Rodriguez, 2019).
To accommodate such growth, the number of gyms, fitness centers, studios, and fitness professionals who run and staff these establishments has exploded. The Bureau of Labor Statistics (2020) projects 15% growth in employment for fitness trainers and instructors from 2019 to 2029 (Figure: Employment Growth Within the Fitness Industry), creating a level of competition that forces fitness professionals to become creative with strategies for setting their business apart.
FIGUREEmployment Growth Within the Fitness IndustryJacob Lund/Shutterstock.comFor fitness professionals looking to diversify, sustain, and grow their book of business (list of clients), offering virtual coaching services can help positively impact these efforts. Recent events have forced clients to seek at-home fitness solutions. Combined with the continuous rise of chronic health conditions like obesity, diabetes, heart disease, and other lifestyle-influenced conditions, this has created an environment in which the demand for adaptable fitness and nutrition coaching services is high.
With the ability to book any client, anywhere, at any time, virtual coaches can make a substantial impact on the health and wellness of clients at practically any fitness level. On any given day, a virtual coach can meet with a weight-loss client in California, train a youth athlete in Michigan, and a lead a group fitness class in Louisiana, all before lunch. There has never been a better or more exciting time for certified fitness professionals to build a virtual coaching business.
Coach's CornerOffering virtual coaching services may be incredibly overwhelming at first. However, fitness professionals should not let the fear of the unknown stop them from taking a positive step toward the game-changing world of virtual coaching. To avoid feeling overwhelmed about the requirements needed to transition the various services offered, the fitness professional should focus on immediate steps they can take to help facilitate this process. For example, before offering virtual coaching sessions with new or existing clients, they should begin by leveraging technology to track and communicate client progress.
Book of businessThe professional’s list of clients.

---

### The Virtual Coach

Chapter 1Introduction to Virtual CoachingLesson 1Welcome to NASM Virtual CoachingThe Virtual CoachVirtual coaches provide live, one-on-one programming through virtual means, such as video, chat, and livestreams, while integrating other communication technologies such as e-mail, file sharing, etc. Much like in-person fitness professionals, virtual coaches meet with clients on a set schedule, work together to set goals and milestones, and discuss progress and potential adjustments to programming.
Le Panda/Shutterstock.comWhile predesigned, one-size-fits-all workouts may be provided, this approach should complement the virtual experience not replace it. When executed effectively, the virtual coaching experience allows the client and virtual coach a more accessible way to work together to achieve goals in a healthy, attainable, realistic, and safe manner.
History of Virtual Coachinghafakot/Shutterstock.comWhile the concept of virtual coaching is relatively new, the use of technology to bring personalized and more private fitness solutions to the masses is not. Currently, virtual coaching is a natural evolution of the virtual training system, which used emerging technology to deliver personal trainer-like services to those who experienced barriers to frequenting a fitness center.

"With the advent of smartphones and affordable sensing technologies, the possibility to automate several of the functions of a personal trainer emerged… [allowing] a person to manage their own personal fitness program while integrating close monitoring, sports science knowledge, and motivational aspects (Lowe & ÓLaighin, 2012)."

Using a combination of smartphone apps, sensing technologies, and image processing devices to gather data before, during, and after workouts, virtual coaches can collect and use client data to coach their clients to success. However, while these technologies make it easy for individuals to track fitness data on their own, they lack the ability to address many of the unforeseen obstacles otherwise addressed by a virtual coach.
Monkey Business Images/Shutterstock.comVirtual coachA professional who provides live, one-on-one programming through virtual means such as video, chat, and livestreams while integrating other communication technologies such as e-mail, file sharing, etc.
Emerging technologyNew technologies currently developing that are expected to have a significant social and/or economic impact.
Sensing technologiesDevices that use sensors to detect and record physical or biological information and often are referred to as biometric wearables.

---

### Trends in Virtual Coaching

Chapter 1Introduction to Virtual CoachingLesson 1Welcome to NASM Virtual CoachingTrends in Virtual CoachingThe limits to virtual coaching extend beyond personal training and nutrition coaching. Remote sessions are now considered a mainstream way of doing business and have become part of the service offerings provided by fitness professionals looking to grow and expand their reach in the industry. Because many technologies used to operate as a virtual coach are now user-friendly for both the client and coach, it is now common to see fitness professionals adopting and integrating virtual strategies within their current in-person training methods, thereby creating a hybrid approach to training and coaching their conventional client base. A virtual coach should assess trends and emerging technologies to determine if they are right for the services they offer and, most importantly, for their client. Included are a few trends in the fitness industry’s virtual workspace that have helped define and differentiate the degrees of operating as a virtual coach.
Predesigned WorkoutsPredesigned workouts, sometimes called canned workouts, are already a part of the fitness professional’s arsenal of tools used to train or coach clients. However, the strategies used to leverage these have drastically evolved in the virtual space. Whether provided as a free preview, included as part of a virtual coaching package, or available for purchase, the ability to provide predesigned workouts via e-mail, file sharing, or website is a great way to show prospective and current clients the fitness professional’s ability to program for various fitness goals. Plus, predesigned workouts can also help virtual coaches expand their book of business or create supplemental income. And while convenient for coaches and clients alike, virtual coaches should avoid using predesigned workouts as a means to minimize or otherwise substitute for real-time interactions during a virtual coaching session with a client.
One-on-One Personal Training and Group FitnessIt has become increasingly easy for fitness professionals to acquire and/or transition many of their clients to virtual sessions with the availability of emerging technologies. Audio/video has become widely used to facilitate real-time communication, even for those who use Smartphones and computers to conduct business with clients who might not be as technologically savvy. Finding more efficient ways to coach more clients should be a goal for fitness professionals looking to grow their abilities while providing an array of service offerings to their clients. Virtual sessions provide fitness professionals with the ability to enjoy high-quality interactions with their clients regardless of location. Whether the need is to provide one-on-one training or instruct a group fitness session, fitness professionals are leveraging the virtual environment while upholding the benefits clients would otherwise have with them in person (Figure: Virtual Personal Training and Group Fitness Instruction).
FIGUREVirtual Personal Training and Group Fitness InstructionOne-on-one training, delivered virtually and in real time, provides clients with the freedom to access a coach that they know, like, and trust from the comfort and privacy of their own home, creating a client-coach relationship that supports successful, long-lasting change.

Virtual coaching for group fitness instruction provides flexibility for clients who cannot make it to an in-person class due to closures, proximity, or personal preference. Various methods of streaming will now accommodate numerous participants in one class, providing virtual coaches with the ability to encourage interaction, build community, and increase accountability.

---

### Check-Ins

Chapter 1Introduction to Virtual CoachingLesson 1Welcome to NASM Virtual CoachingCheck-InsScheduling regular client check-ins is a crucial step to ensuring client engagement as they continue to work toward their fitness goals. However, it may become increasingly difficult to require clients to allocate the time required to have these check-in discussions with their coaches. For fitness professionals, integrating virtual technologies to help make it more feasible for their clients to check in, while remote, is an effective way to hold clients accountable to their fitness goals. In a 2017 study aimed at discovering how fitness technology impacts behavior change, researchers found that:

“Interventions that included established behavior change techniques were associated with greater increases in physical activity and subsequent weight loss than those that did not. Specifically, self-regulatory behavior change techniques such as goal setting, self-monitoring, and social support were associated with better outcomes (Sullivan & Lachman, 2017).”

Fitness professionals use goal setting as a tool to motivate clients, and the exercise is especially important in a virtual environment where another key factor for success, social support, is not physically present. Goals are often tracked in a health and fitness app, but virtual check-ins complement virtual coaching by adding accountability, consistency, and a sense of support to the client/coach relationship.
Nutrition CoachingVirtual nutrition coaching allows fitness professionals to connect with clients in new and useful ways. Using a virtual environment, a nutrition coach has the ability to take a virtual tour of a client’s kitchen to review pantry items, discuss food labels, check snack options on-hand, talk about using certain kitchen tools to maximize efficiency and meal prep, or to brainstorm alternatives based on what a client already has in the kitchen. Having the ability to leverage technology to get a peek at a client’s living space has become a powerful strategy to helping clients identify obstacles and barriers otherwise overlooked when trying to improve eating habits.
Try ThisNext time a client cannot make an in-person session work, reschedule with a virtual session instead of cancelling altogether. Having the ability to provide clients an alternative option can help address and overcome client objections.

---

### Clients Seeking Virtual Coaching

Chapter 1Introduction to Virtual CoachingLesson 1Welcome to NASM Virtual CoachingClients Seeking Virtual CoachingVirtual coaching is a benefit for existing and prospective clients alike, but it will not be for every everyone. Fitness professionals looking to offer virtual coaching to new or existing clients will need to understand whether a client will be successful engaging with them in a virtual environment compared to traditional in-person training and/or coaching methods. During conversations with existing or prospective clients, it is important to ask them questions that will help determine their interest level in being trained or coached using a virtual environment and/or engaging with various virtual technologies. In most cases, the fitness professional may uncover clients who want virtual coaching services but lack the understanding and experience to determine whether it is an effective solution for their fitness goals. When identifying and discussing options with clients who may benefit from virtual coaching services, it is important to understand that offering virtual coaching services is not an all-or-nothing or one-size-fits-all approach. Instead, it can be used to help facilitate the fitness professional’s coach/client relationship, promoting goal attainment while minimizing sales objections. 
goodluz/Shutterstock.comThe Client Who Prefers to Stay HomeIt is no surprise that there are clients who avoid the gym environment due to any number of reasons including long commutes, packed schedules, family commitments, and/or even lack of self-confidence. Regardless of why these individuals do not frequent the gym, it is the responsibility of the fitness professional to overcome these obstacles. The use of virtual coaching can help bridge the gap for individuals who are looking for change but may lack the desire to participate in training or coaching in a gym. No matter the reason, virtual coaching allows these clients to experience the benefits of health and fitness programming with live guidance from a fitness professional. 
The Client Who Moved AwayTypically, when a client moves away it ends the client/trainer relationship. However, virtual coaching allows the fitness professional to leverage the virtual environment to continue their relationship through remote training and/or coaching. With a few adjustments to accommodate available space, access to equipment, and scheduling, clients who move away may find it easier to continue their current programming or coaching sessions versus finding another trainer or coach.

---

### Clients Who Vacation Frequently

Chapter 1Introduction to Virtual CoachingLesson 1Welcome to NASM Virtual CoachingClients Who Vacation FrequentlyClients who skip training or nutrition coaching sessions on extended vacations often experience a momentum shift and risk derailing the efforts previously made to reach their goals. Virtual coaches can bridge that gap by suggesting live sessions that accommodate equipment and space changes or providing predesigned workouts while clients are on vacation. Depending on the client’s goals, these sessions could be lighter than usual, incorporate local activities, and focus more on recovery than progression.
Clients With Demanding Work SchedulesClients whose professions require shift work or exhaustive work schedules that do not accommodate normal working hours often have difficulty sticking to a schedule, making it hard to provide consistent fitness solutions, whether in-person or virtual. According to the World Health Organization (2020), “adults aged 18-24 should do at least 150 minutes of moderate-intensity aerobic physical activity throughout the week” no matter their work schedule. Because of this, virtual coaching is a great opportunity for those that may not have been able to make the traditional gym hours required for in-person training or coaching work. While working with such clients may require virtual coach to accommodate odd hours for training, the impact could be immense. A 2018 study found that “many shift workers fail to achieve the recommended physical activity guidelines and [,] consequently, the majority of shift workers are insufficiently active” (Flahr et al., 2018).
Global Pandemic Restrictions (COVID-19)When gyms and health clubs were forced to close due to COVD-19, virtual coaching allowed fitness professionals to quickly pivot to a safe and effective environment, while maintaining or even growing the business. While the fitness industry focused on keeping businesses afloat, health care providers emphasized the connection between physical fitness and recovery from the virus, noting that “exercise may reduce the risk of acute respiratory distress syndrome, a major cause of death in patients with coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19)” (Nyenhuis, et al., 2020). This connection reinforces the need for virtual coaching as a means of educating clients on the importance of physical fitness as a part of a comprehensive proactive health management program.
The potential client types and opportunities identified are merely examples of those who may benefit most from virtual coaching. By appropriately identifying and integrating various virtual technologies with specific needs, virtual coaching can benefit just about any client, solidifying the service as one of the most versatile and innovative ways to make an impact on the fitness industry as a professional.

---

### Qualifications for Virtual Coaching

Chapter 1Introduction to Virtual CoachingLesson 2Virtual Coaching ConsiderationsQualifications for Virtual CoachingThe qualifications of a virtual coach can be interpreted in many ways based on the professional’s education and industry experience, as well as the character traits required to sustain success as a virtual coach. Aside from working with clients, it is imperative proper credentials have been obtained to safely and effectively coach clients with various health and fitness goals. Without the foundational knowledge and credentialing in either fitness training and/or nutrition coaching, the risk of client injury and liability is greatly increased. Becoming a virtual coach should help augment the professional’s credentials recognized by the fitness industry and is not intended as a substitute.
Fitness CredentialsThe transition to virtual coaching should be relatively smooth for an experienced personal trainer or nutrition coach looking to broaden services offered to clients. Although not required, those new to the fitness industry should consider completing the NASM-CPT or NASM-CNC course before venturing into the virtual coaching environment.
Character Traits of a Successful Virtual CoachA fitness professional’s passion for motivating and helping clients to learn, grow, and achieve goals should be evident through virtual coaching strategies. When transitioning or augmenting current services, fitness professionals may face communication challenges such as maintaining appropriate tone and cadence, building rapport, and maintaining a coach–client relationship that encourages accountability. When looking to overcome these challenges, consistency is key.
Having an entrepreneurial spirit is another essential attribute for virtual coaches who look to attract and retain clients while minimizing turnover. The virtual coach’s proactive mindset keeps them seeking innovative ways to reach new clients and improve their ability to provide effective coaching. This entrepreneurial mindset can help drive the virtual coach to be creative with marketing, social media presence, networking, referral programs, innovative promotion ideas, and gated content to bring in new clients. Without this continuous drive for creativity and innovation, it becomes increasingly difficult to stand out in the virtual setting.

---

### Scope of Practice

Chapter 1Introduction to Virtual CoachingLesson 2Virtual Coaching ConsiderationsScope of PracticeVirtual coaches are expected to adhere to the scope of practice, which includes the procedures and processes fitness professionals follow in accordance with their fitness certification.
After evaluating a client’s situation thoroughly, honestly, and independently, virtual coaches will be required to determine whether it is safe for the client to provide virtual coaching services or if a referral to an appropriate medical professional is needed. Whether the virtual coach is providing training or nutrition coaching, the scope of practice resides with the credentials carried by the professional. The limitations and boundaries of the coaching services a fitness professional is able to provide can be found within their credentials already obtained. While operating in a virtual environment does not change the scope of practice, it can provide some additional challenges.
Critical!When offering virtual coaching services to clients, fitness professionals should review the scope of practice limitations and boundaries identified by the credentials earned through the organization or certifying body from which they were obtained. Whether the professional is offering training services or nutrition coaching, offering these services in a virtual format does not change the scope of practice.
Certified Personal Training Personal trainers looking to use the virtual environment as an additional service for their clients will need to adhere to the scope of practice limitations and boundaries they operate with within their in-person training sessions. Because of the limits of operating virtually, the personal trainer will still be required to identify when it is appropriate to refer the client to a licensed professional.

    Personal trainers do not diagnose injuries. If a client sustains an injury during a training session, the trainer should immediately refer the client to the appropriate licensed medical professional. This protocol is for physical and psychological diagnoses alike.
    Personal trainers do not provide counseling services for past or present. If approached by a client seeking advice for a medical issue or injury, the trainer should immediately refer the client to the appropriate licensed medical professional.

Certified Nutrition Coaching Professionals with the opportunity to provide nutrition coaching services understand the potential impact on their client’s health through their ability to provide education on making healthy choices and promoting positive behavior change. Like the certified personal training professional, nutrition coaches looking to offer virtual services will be required to adhere to their scope of practice and refer out to a licensed professional when necessary.

    Nutrition coaches do not provide meal planning services. If approached by a client requesting meal planning, the nutrition coach should immediately refer the client to a registered dietitian.
    Nutrition coaches do not provide nutritional therapy. If approached by a client seeking nutritional therapy, the nutrition coach should immediately refer the client to the appropriate licensed medical professional.  

Scope of practiceThe actions that a professional is permitted to undertake in keeping with the terms of her or his license or certification.

---

### Virtual Coaching Obstacles and Challenges

Chapter 1Introduction to Virtual CoachingLesson 2Virtual Coaching ConsiderationsVirtual Coaching Obstacles and ChallengesWhile virtual coaching is a timely solution to an industry-wide problem, fitness professionals should understand and acknowledge the constraints that come with providing virtual services. Compared to in-person services, such as personal training or nutrition coaching, the fitness professional looking to augment their services by offering a virtual option will need to address additional obstacles and challenges faced in the virtual environment. Aside from the technical obstacles and challenges faced when offering virtual coaching, the virtual coach will need to identify and acknowledge that there are differences between interacting in-person versus virtually with the client.
insta_photos/Shutterstock.comClient EngagementA virtual environment makes it easier for clients to disengage, making client retention markedly more difficult for virtual coaches. Therefore, fitness professionals looking to offer virtual services will face new challenges with client engagement. Unlike in-person personal training or nutrition coaching, the virtual coach will need to seek creative solutions to ensure client engagement is upheld consistently throughout each virtual session, not to mention the required commitment for clients to complete offline activities such as forms, logs, etc. Understanding how to gauge and measure a client’s engagement as a virtual coach is a crucial skill to providing effective virtual coaching. Because every client engages differently, the virtual coach will need to pay greater attention to how their client reacts to the virtual coaching they are provided.

---

### Client Safety

Chapter 1Introduction to Virtual CoachingLesson 2Virtual Coaching ConsiderationsClient SafetyLike with in-person personal training and nutrition coaching, the client’s safety should remain a top priority. When offering virtual services, it is imperative that fitness professionals design programs that prioritize client safety while being conducive to the virtual environment. Because the virtual coach is unable to be with their client, they should continuously assess and address the client when risk of injury is identified during a live session.
When executing a virtual training session with a client, fitness professionals should keep the following in mind to help minimize injury risk:


    Virtual coaches cannot see every angle of the client.
    Virtual coaches cannot act as a spotter and should not provide programming that requires one.
    Virtual coaches should be more diligent about monitoring and correcting form, demonstrating exercises, and watching for safety concerns such as abnormal discomfort.
    Virtual coaches should never assume their clients’ abilities.

Critical!In addition to programming considerations, virtual coaches must be prepared to react quickly, notifying the client’s local authorities and/or an emergency contact should something go wrong during the virtual training session. For this reason, each client should provide a street address, emergency contact, and local police department phone number.
Professional BurnoutWorking remotely in any profession comes with the challenge of achieving a healthy work–life balance. For fitness professionals who are just beginning with virtual coaching, earning new clients can be exciting; however, packing a schedule with sessions every hour on the hour can quickly lead to burnout. Considering that work and life will probably never be balanced, a 2014 study on the work habits of surgeons suggests the concept of creating a work–life budget.

"Just as a budget is made at the beginning of each year for… the home, a budget should be made for work and life. And just as importantly, the budget for work–life balance needs to be individualized to best meet the needs of the individual (Raja & Stein, 2014)."

That being said, virtual coaches should set working hours while reserving time for personal and professional development. Setting these boundaries can help virtual coaches stay healthy while setting a positive example for clients.

---

### Summary

Chapter 1Introduction to Virtual CoachingLesson 2Virtual Coaching ConsiderationsSummaryVirtual coaching allows fitness professionals to serve clients that may have otherwise been out of reach due to their preference, proximity, or availability. On the surface, it seems like the logical next step for every fitness professional, but virtual coaching will not work for everyone. Just as a fitness professional examines a client’s needs before assigning a training program, a similar assessment must be completed to determine if adding virtual training is a sound business decision. When determining whether to offer virtual services, fitness professionals should consider the following pros and cons of virtual coaching:
Twinsterphoto/Shutterstock.comPros
    Allows any fitness professional to reach a global audience
    Can be more affordable for clients than in-person training (especially without a gym membership and travel considerations), increasing the potential of acquiring new clients
    More opportunities in geographical locations with few fitness facilities to work at
    Same coach for clients no matter the location, providing the opportunity to build greater, longer lasting client–coach relationships

Cons
    Clients lacking understanding for the value of virtual coaching and preferring in-person training or coaching, making it hard to sell the benefits of virtual services
    Fitness goals requiring the fitness professional to be present with the client to minimize the risk of injury
    Technology obstacles such as Internet connection, device requirements, and technical support sometimes making it difficult to ensure a seamless and effective virtual session
    Allocating appropriate space for virtual sessions challenging for fitness professionals who do not or cannot stage a designated personal training or coaching space in their home

Successful fitness professionals are committed to helping their clients realize the benefits of achieving specific fitness and nutrition goals. When incorporating virtual services into their business model, the fitness professional has the potential of taking this client commitment one step further.
While it may seem that virtual training can take away from the personal and professional experience shared between a coach and client, one can also argue that it does just the opposite. Seeking additional ways to support clients’ needs in this way will add to the level of care, understanding, and respect provided as virtual services to clients daily. In addition, there has never been a better (or more workable) time in history to augment the services offered by fitness professionals leveraging a virtual strategy. As technology and online business continues to grow, the normalcy of meeting and conducting business online has become commonplace. Having the knowledge and skills to offer virtual solutions at a global scale is not only lucrative and smart for the growing professional, but also incredibly beneficial for clients who otherwise would not have the opportunity to engage in traditional coaching.

# Chapter 2: Building a Business

### Chapter 2 - Building a Business

Chapter 2Building a BusinessLesson 1The Virtual Coaching BusinessChapter 2 - Building a BusinessBuilding a successful virtual coaching business is not as simple as setting up a video conferencing account, launching a website, and being passionate about helping clients. A sustainable business requires strategic planning and considerations that extend beyond having knowledge of exercise science principles. This chapter will provide information related to financial forecasting, budgeting, bookkeeping, sales and marketing, and how to differentiate a virtual coaching business in a competitive marketplace.
Learning ObjectivesAfter completing this chapter, the NASM Virtual Coach will have the ability to do the following:


    Identify qualities of an entrepreneur. 
    Determine the key components in a business plan.
    Write a virtual coaching business plan.

---

### Introduction

Chapter 2Building a BusinessLesson 1The Virtual Coaching BusinessIntroductionBuilding a successful business requires time, perseverance, hard work, and patience, but the rewards are limitless! When the fitness professional is able to build a business, they create an opportunity to provide a life and the freedom they desire. Building a business requires a different skill set from simply performing a job. It often requires a wide range of personal, technical, and financial skills as well as entrepreneurial spirit.

One key step to building a successful business is developing a carefully crafted business plan. A business plan can help provide a blueprint and a structured plan that will help the fitness professional build a successful, thriving business.
Have a nice day Photo/Shutterstock.comBusiness plans will be specific to each business and business owner as business owners will have many different goals—both personal and professional. For example, a business model for a company that will provide the owner and his family with a comfortable living for generations is different from a business plan that will provide the owner with the ability to work with flexible hours, an additional household income, and travel freely. Just as personal goals can change over time, a business’s purpose and direction can change overtime as well. Developing a living business plan that is carefully thought out, well-crafted, and routinely updated will allow the fitness professional to build a business that fits their vision for both personal and professional success.
This chapter will provide an overview of the purpose of a virtual coaching business, the qualities of entrepreneurship, and how to build a business plan. The chapter will identify the mission of the business as well as address marketing the business, selling the business’s services or products, and how to manage the financial aspects of the business.

---

### Purpose of a Virtual Coaching Business

Chapter 2Building a BusinessLesson 1The Virtual Coaching BusinessPurpose of a Virtual Coaching BusinessOnline businesses have been around for over 30 years and businesses of all sizes now operate solely online. Businesses that were once only found in brick-and-mortar locations are now becoming more popular in an online marketplace rather than in physical locations. Groceries, shoes, and even new film releases are all presented to consumers from the comfort of their home. The world has shifted to an acceptance of and reliance on virtual commerce.
In the current market, there are many unknowns that turn into high risks for a new business. The risk of an economic downturn is always on the mind of a successful entrepreneur. When sales slow down, a business still has employees and bills to pay. Building a successful business that is dependent on someone else (e.g., training clients at a gym someone else owns) does not provide the entrepreneur with complete control over their success. Similarly, building a business with large overhead not only requires massive amounts of startup capital, but also places the fitness professional under financial stress during periods of slow economic growth.
Food for ThoughtIn 2019, the virtual fitness coaching industry was valued at over $6 billion and is expected to reach almost $60 billion by 2027 (Rake & Gaikwad, 2020).
dmitriy-orlovskiy/Shutterstock.comVirtual coaching businesses have existed in various forms for many years but have recently seen substantial growth as more consumers are becoming comfortable with, and even preferring, online coaching. This industry is at a tipping point that is starting to disrupt the in-person training industry, which has dominated the personal training market since its inception. Fitness professionals are becoming more comfortable with virtual coaching, which provides more opportunities for them to start their own business. Virtual coaches can start with little overhead and still work as an in-person fitness professional while building up their virtual coaching startup to a self-sustaining, thriving online business.

As the world shifts to an ever-larger virtual presence, clients are looking for ways to integrate health and fitness into the virtual world. Fitness professionals must find a path to meet the demand for virtual services that not only provides the best training for clients, but also provides the same, or greater, job stability that in-person training offers.

---

### Qualities of an Entrepreneur

Chapter 2Building a BusinessLesson 1The Virtual Coaching BusinessQualities of an EntrepreneurThe Internet has made being an entrepreneur or business owner more accessible. The terms entrepreneur and business owner are often used interchangeably. However, there are differences between the two (Marks, 2012; Spring, 2014). In fact, over 18% of small businesses do not see themselves as entrepreneurs (Huefner & Hunt, 1998).
Small business owners are typically local businesses with one or a few physical locations within a small geographic area. They are often well-known throughout the community as a business owner and have deep personal connections with their customers. A business owner typically spends most of their time working in their business instead of on their business. Entrepreneurs are often small business owners as well; however, sometimes entrepreneurs may enter a new business but not own it. Other times, an entrepreneur may be in between businesses after selling theirs.
Food for ThoughtWorking in the business includes training clients, responding to customer service e-mails, and bookkeeping.

Working on the business includes developing training protocols, creating and testing marketing strategies, crafting customer service policies, structuring corporate guidelines, and building financial projections.

In the early stages of a business, the business owner will be focused primarily on working in the business. However, as a business grows, the entrepreneur will need to shift much of their focus to working on the business.
EntrepreneurA business owner who develops systematic, repeatable, scalable, and teachable processes to grow a business.
Business ownerAn individual or group who owns a business entity and has final decision-making authority as well as first right to profits

---

### Content

Chapter 2Building a BusinessLesson 1The Virtual Coaching BusinessConsider the qualities of an entrepreneur:


    Has a vision of the future: looking not just at the next week, but the next month, quarter, year, decade
    Continues to test and modify their business until they achieve their vision
    Understands how to share their vision with others
    Wants full control over their destiny
    Enjoys a challenge
    Takes risk: Being able to take risks is the most important and common trait of entrepreneurs (Cramer et al., 2002). Taking calculated risk, but not unnecessary risk, can vastly improve the odds of becoming a successful entrepreneur.
    Spends equal or more time working on their business versus working in their business

pathdoc/Shutterstock.com

### Introduction to a Virtual Coaching Business Plan

Chapter 2Building a BusinessLesson 2Building a Virtual Coaching Business Plan: Part IIntroduction to a Virtual Coaching Business PlanA business plan is the instruction manual for how the business will grow, which outlines the path the fitness professional will follow to achieve their overall vision. As an instruction manual, a business plan is a living document that must be reviewed, updated, and rewritten as a business evolves and changes. Having a business plan gives the fitness professional a place to put their thoughts into words and gives them the ability to easily share their vision with others.
The length and detail of the fitness professional’s business plan can and will vary depending on several factors. If the new entrepreneur is seeking to take out a bank loan or take on partners or investors, they will need to go into greater detail on financial information than they would if they were starting a business on their own. If the business owner is a solopreneur (an entrepreneur with no employees or partners), then the goal of the business plan is to have a guide to keep the fitness professional on course while building their business.
Please note: After each description of the different sections of the business plan are at least three Business Plan Questions to help the fitness professional develop their own well-thought-out and detailed business plan. These prompts will be followed by an example of properly developed answers that will be used to formulate a final business plan, which will be completed at the end of this chapter.
An example company, called Executive Fitness, will be created to demonstrate what well-thought-out answers to the business plan writing prompts should look like. The example company’s goal is to provide online fitness coaching to busy office professionals who do not have time to visit a gym on a consistent basis. The example company will be expanded on as the business plan is developed further in this chapter.
The Business Plan Questions should be completed after reading the entire chapter because many of these questions and ideas work and build off of each other. In order to develop a complete picture of what the fitness professional’s virtual coaching business will look like, the fitness professional must understand all of the concepts in this chapter first.
Food for ThoughtA business plan should be treated as a living document. It should be reviewed, referenced, and updated regularly.
Business planA document that outlines how a business will be structured, grow, and achieve goals that are set forth by the business owner or executive team.
SolopreneurAn entrepreneur who runs a business on their own.

---

### Executive Summary

Chapter 2Building a BusinessLesson 2Building a Virtual Coaching Business Plan: Part IExecutive SummaryZurainy Zain/Shutterstock.comThe first section of a business plan is the executive summary. In the executive summary, the fitness professional will describe the purpose of the business. This part is the who, what, and why of the business.

    Who are the founders and key people in the business? Who is the business targeting? What other stakeholders should be considered?
    What makes this business different, more effective, and, most importantly, unique?
    Why are clients going to come to this new, untested, and unproven business?

Each section of the executive summary can expand over time into their own sections of the business plan as the fitness professional develops more experience and a more detailed path for bringing their overall vision for the business to fruition.
Mission and Vision StatementsAs an entrepreneur, the fitness professional is the business’s management team. The fitness professional is, essentially, the director of everything. This is where, as the company’s chief visionary, they will lay the foundation for the future of the company for years to come. The fitness professional should discuss where they see the business in 1, 5, and 10 years along with the milestones of how they plan to get to each of those goals. They will discuss the driving force behind their business and their company's vision and mission statement.
Tang Yan Song/Shutterstock.comA mission statement is what drives the business’s culture and progress over time. A mission statement is the entrepreneur’s reason for wanting to start this business and should convey a meaning that resonates (and motivates) with the founder, employees, and clients (Bart, 1997).

A vision statement is what the business will look like in the long-term, over the next 5 to 10 years or more. If the overall goals of the business are met, the vision statement should describe an outside, broad view of the company.
Executive summaryA section of the business plan that provides an overview of what will be covered in the document.
Mission statementA mission statement is the entrepreneur’s reason for wanting to start this business and should convey a meaning that resonates (and motivates) with the founder, employees, and clients.
Vision statementWhat the business will look like in the long-term, over the next 5 to 10 years or more.

---

### Business Plan Questions

Chapter 2Building a BusinessLesson 2Building a Virtual Coaching Business Plan: Part IBusiness Plan Questions
    Who is the target audience? What challenge does the target audience face that the business can help solve?
    What is the fitness professional’s personal connection and motivation to help solve this problem?
    What does the business look like in 1, 5, and 10 years?

Executive Fitness Example1. Who is the target audience? What challenge does the target audience face that the business can help solve?

The target audience is busy business professionals who want to give their health, fitness, and nutrition a higher priority but have difficulty finding a sustainable path to achieve their goals.
2. What is the fitness professional’s personal connection and motivation to help solve this problem?

This business is owned by someone who, prior to becoming a fitness professional, worked a corporate office job. Firsthand experience with the lack of personal health goals in accomplished individuals is vast. Balancing a 40+-hour work week with family life, being sedentary, lacking motivation to exercise, and not understanding there is a sustainable path to health are all common realities. This allows for empathy with clients and for developing a plan that will fit their aspirations.
3. What does the business look like in 1, 5, and 10 years?

During the first year, systems will be developed and refined while the company services the professional market the owner is most familiar with—those working larger corporate jobs in upper to middle management. After the first year, using feedback from clients combined with additional market research, the company will be able to evolve and expand the service to other professionals in different markets, such as teachers and educators of all levels. The company will also start to bring on employees at that time to ensure high quality of service is maintained. At the 5-year mark, the business will have started partnering with corporations to provide in-house wellness services to employees, as well as continuing to grow the direct-to-client coaching services. At the 10-year mark, the business will be a large company in the online training industry, generating millions of dollars of revenue annually. This growth will require an entire professional management team, along with a strong corporate structure and a constantly updated and refined business plan.

---

### Company Description

Chapter 2Building a BusinessLesson 2Building a Virtual Coaching Business Plan: Part ICompany DescriptionA description of the company should be concise yet detailed. Simply stating that a business will conduct online personal training is too vague. Conversely, going into detail about what estimated customer acquisition cost (CAC) will be is too in-depth for the executive summary (this will be included in expanded sections of the business plan such as marketing or financial). A description of the business should discuss the purpose of the business and what makes it unique. The uniqueness is often described as the unique selling proposition (USP). This is the place that the fitness professional should explain why customers will choose their business over a competitor.
Highlight the true uniqueness of the business. Mentioning features of a business are simply not enough. There was a time when responding to an e-mail within 24 hours or offering text/messaging to a client was unique and rare. However, now that there are more online coaching businesses, these features have become the standard. The fitness professional needs a USP that only they bring to their clients.
niroworld/Shutterstock.comIt is also a good idea, and common practice, to include information on any of the company founders, along with their backgrounds. Even if the business owner is a solopreneur, they should be able to sell themselves and explain why their background and experience makes them the most suited to bring their USP to the market.
The company description should also include the management structure of the company. If the business will be starting with partners or employees, their roles and responsibilities should be clearly defined here. Highlight why these individuals will be vital to the business’s initial success. If these employees are not included in the description and backgrounds of the company founders, then they should be included here.
Most entrepreneurs are solopreneurs at first. They are the only employee in the business. This means they are working both in and on the business. Take this opportunity to clarify what roles everyone involved will be taking on and what roles may be contracted out. For instance, most entrepreneurs will take on marketing, but few will do their own taxes.
Helpful HintThere are many gig services, companies that connect consumers or businesses with freelancers to perform various tasks or jobs, that can be found online for minimal cost. These can be utilized for things like creating infographics, editing blogs, and editing videos.

It is also possible to find reputable and affordable online services for things like accounting and legal advice.
Customer acquisition cost (CAC)How much a new customer costs a business – typically, the cost is determined through money spent on advertising.
Unique selling proposition (USP)The unique benefit or feature of a product or service that differentiates a business from other businesses offering a similar service or product.

---

### Business Plan Questions

Chapter 2Building a BusinessLesson 2Building a Virtual Coaching Business Plan: Part IBusiness Plan Questions
    What are the unique goals of the business?
    What makes the company unique to clients? What single thing does the business perform or understand better than anyone else in the industry?
    Who will the company consist of at inception? What roles will each person have?

Executive Fitness Example1. What are the short-term and long-term goals of the business?

The company’s short-term goals include developing systems to provide customized, but repeatable client experiences. This will help to achieve the long-term goal of increasing coaches and to provide sustainable, high quality, and personalized online fitness coaching to a growing number of clients with a well-trained staff.

2. What makes the company unique for clients? What single thing does the business perform or understand better than anyone else in the industry?

As business owners that had executive careers prior to transitioning to a career in fitness, any future trainers brought on board will also have that experience of having found the balance between work, family, and health. That is an experience that cannot be taught but must be learned to be appreciated. This will allow the trainers to empathize with and understand the client’s needs, wants, limitations, and goals better than anyone else in the industry.

3. Who will the company consist of at inception? What roles will each person have?

At inception, the company will consist of one owner-operator with goals to bring on up to four additional online trainers during a 2-year period. For the first year, the owner will balance their time between marketing, product development, sales, and servicing clients. At any time the owner feels overwhelmed in a specific area, help will be sought out in that specific area.

---

### Identify Target Audience

Chapter 2Building a BusinessLesson 2Building a Virtual Coaching Business Plan: Part IIdentify Target AudienceThe final section of the Executive Summary in the business plan should identify who the clients will be. Most business owners already have some idea of who their target audience is. Having too wide a target audience, the business’s message will get lost and not resonate with clients. A narrow target audience and will be too specific for anyone to hear the message. The target audience should be someone the fitness professional can relate to, that they know their USP will help, and that they feel will provide the business with enough clients to grow and expand.

Realize that every business will have clients who are outside of their target audience and their target audience will change over time, especially in the early stages of the business. For example, some fitness professionals may start out only wanting to work with competitive physique athletes, but quickly find out they prefer working with seniors who are trying to improve their quality of life.
Jirsack/Shutterstock.comBusiness Plan Questions
    What is the ideal client’s goal?
    Where does the ideal client live?
    How old is the ideal client?
    How long is the average client enrolled in coaching for?
    Are there any other specifics about the ideal client?

Executive Fitness Example1. What is the ideal client’s goal?

The ideal client’s goal is not just to lose weight or get into shape. The ideal client’s goal is to find a balance between life, work, and physical health.

2. Where does the ideal client live?

In order to provide prompt service to clients and properly manage the limited time a small business owner or business professional has, the ideal client is located in North America. Exact geographical location outside of that does not matter, as this is a virtual coaching company.

3. How old is the ideal client?

The ideal client is between the ages of 30 and 45 and is most likely settling into their second job in the same profession.

4. How long is the average client enrolled in coaching for?

Based on previous experience with in-person training and nutrition clients, the average client will need to be enrolled in the coaching program for at least 3 months before achieving their goals and possessing the knowledge to manage their own nutrition and training.

5. Are there any other specifics about the ideal client?

Based on both personal and professional experiences, the ideal client has one to two children under the age of 10. Experience in finding ways to balance training with family life, in addition to work, allows the company to bring a unique and sought-after service to clients.

---

### Products and Services

Chapter 2Building a BusinessLesson 2Building a Virtual Coaching Business Plan: Part IProducts and ServicesOnline Personal Training is not a product per se, but rather a service. The product description in the business plan must include detailed descriptions of what features will be brought to the market and what makes the product or service unique. Products and/or services should always be improving and changing. Offering the same service with no improvements is a quick way to become irrelevant in the highly competitive landscape of businesses, especially with online businesses. With that said, the downfall of many entrepreneurs is trying to perfect their product too much prior to actually having real-world feedback.
This section of the business plan should focus on the minimum viable product (MVP) that the business will be starting with. Remember the business plan is a living document that can be modified and updated as product offerings change. There are many approaches to developing an MVP. In this section, it will be broken down step-by-step to develop multiple product offerings that can get to market quickly and are easy to improve based on feedback from customers.
Helpful HintBefore starting to develop the MVP, do some simple market research and gain insight to what future competitors are doing. If the top competitors of the business are known, browse those companies’ websites, e-mail the companies with questions, and read their content. If the business’s competitors are unknown, search online for keywords that potential clients would most likely use to find services the business is going to offer. This is not to steal anything from competitors but, rather, to ensure the business is offering industry-standard items. The fitness professional may even find that what all competitor companies offer is not good enough for their clients and they will have the opportunity to build an industry-changing product.
Minimum viable product (MVP)A product that provides the bare minimum features to satisfy customer demand – this is the foundation for a product that will be developed and modified based on market feedback.

---

### Content

Chapter 2Building a BusinessLesson 2Building a Virtual Coaching Business Plan: Part IThe first step is to develop a base service. This base service forms the foundation for the USP and should be something the target market audience needs, as discussed in the previous section. Building on the example of offering online coaching to busy professionals, the base service will be set as Online Fitness Coaching.
Now that the product has been established, it is time to expand on what that product will actually include. These are the features and benefits. Benefits of a product will be discussed in the sales section of this chapter. For product development, the focus will be on features.
Features are the USP of a business’s services—essentially, what the service is. This is when the fitness professional will use their creativity and passion to accurately describe their product. To illustrate this point, here is an example of some simple features of a common dry-erase marker:

    Bright, vibrant colors
    Ergonomic fit to left or right hand
    Strong, durable casing
    Ability to write on multiple surfaces
    Easily erased
    Secure cap to prevent accidental markings
    Soft felt tip that does not require sharpening
    Disposable, no messy ink to refill

Mtsaride/Shutterstock.comThese features are not unique; they are features shared by almost all dry-erase markers. However, the fitness professional should be able to emphasize not only the uniqueness of their product, but uniquely emphasize the common features of their product. Now, using this strategy to find the features of Online Fitness Coaching, it is possible to develop answers for the example company.

Please note: This is not meant to be an all-inclusive list of what online coaching is or should be but simply a list for the specific example company that is being built.
Features and benefitsFeatures are descriptions of what a product or service offers customers – Benefits are how those features help solve a customer's problem.

---

### Features of Online Fitness Coaching Example

Chapter 2Building a BusinessLesson 2Building a Virtual Coaching Business Plan: Part IFeatures of Online Fitness Coaching Example
    Weekly check-ins with the coach that build toward sustainable fitness
    Custom training programs built around goals
    24/7 e-mail access to the coach with a guaranteed reply within 24 hours
    Access to the client-only newsletter, discussing the latest evidence-based fitness practices
    Coaching designed with flexibility in mind for individuals with busy schedules

This simple list of only five features highlights common offerings in online fitness coaching. However, most online coaches do not promote their common features (1 to 4) and simply highlight their USP (5). A common example of this in other industries is the Schlitz Beer company, which took their product to the top selling beer in the world simply by highlighting how beer was made as their USP. Their technique was standard in the beer industry, but no companies had mentioned how beer was made before Schlitz (Harter, 2018).
Business Plan Questions
    What are two common competitor features that the company might offer clients?
    What are two standard features that other companies are not highlighting?
    Describe the USP in 1 to 2 sentences.

The features have now been created for the product, Online Fitness Coaching. The example company officially has its first product. However, having one product is not good for the longer-term plan of gaining feedback and modifying the product. Therefore, a few offerings should be created based on the core product, Online Fitness Coaching.
Helpful HintFind balance when offering multiple products and services. Not only will the fitness professional put a lot of time and effort into developing products that may not be successful, it may actually hurt sales. Overchoice is when there are so many options in the marketplace that a buyer is overwhelmed and may choose to not purchase anything at all (Settle & Golden, 1974).

---

### Content

Chapter 2Building a BusinessLesson 2Building a Virtual Coaching Business Plan: Part IFirst, take the example company’s base product of Online Fitness Coaching and expanding it into three distinct product offerings:


    Online Exercise Coaching
    Online Nutrition Coaching
    Online Exercise and Nutrition Coaching (which contains both exercise and nutrition coaching)

The company will need to modify its USP or features from the original product to fit these expanded offerings. There will be some crossover and additional features. This is commonly seen in pricing charts, as seen in Table: Online Fitness Coaching Product Features Examples.
Online Fitness Coaching Product Features ExamplesOnline Exercise Coaching
Online Nutrition Coaching
Online Exercise and Nutrition Coaching
Weekly check-ins with the coach that build toward sustainable exercise habits
Weekly check-ins with the coach that build toward sustainable nutrition habits
Weekly check-ins with the coach that build toward sustainable exercise and nutrition habits
Custom exercise training programs built around goals and preferences
Custom nutrition program built around goals and preferences
Custom exercise and nutrition programs built around goals and preferences
24/7 email access to the coach with a guaranteed reply within 24 hours
24/7 email access to the coach with a guaranteed reply within 24 hours
24/7 email access to the coach with a guaranteed reply within 24 hours
Access to the client-only newsletter, discussing the latest evidence-based exercise practices
Access to the client-only newsletter, discussing the latest evidence-based nutrition practices
Access to the client only newsletter, discussing the latest evidence-based exercise and nutrition practices
Coaching designed with flexibility in mind for individuals with busy schedules
Coaching designed with flexibility in mind for individuals with busy schedules
Coaching designed with flexibility in mind for individuals with busy schedules
With this update, there are now three products to offer clients and test in the marketplace. Because of this, there have also been simple upgrades and add-ons developed for clients, which will increase sales. For instance, if a client enrolls in Online Exercise Coaching, but after a consultation with the business owner feels they should also partake in nutrition coaching, it is a simple upsell offer to Complete Online Fitness Coaching. This will also work in reverse to increase customer's lifetime value (CLV). If a client is canceling their services at $150 per month for Complete Online Fitness Coaching, the fitness professional can offer to downgrade the client to either nutrition or exercise coaching at only $100 per month.
Customer lifetime value (CLV)The amount that a customer will spend with business over a lifetime.

---

### Business Plan Questions

Chapter 2Building a BusinessLesson 2Building a Virtual Coaching Business Plan: Part IEach of these three offerings will be further developed to produce quality minimum viable products (MVPs) that will provide product add-ons, options, and a pricing structure that will be competitive in the marketplace.
Business Plan Questions
    What is the core service?
    What are two variations of that service and how do those variations relate to the core service?
    What is one unique feature for each of those new variations?

Executive Fitness Example1. What is the core service?

The core service for Executive Fitness is Complete Online Fitness Coaching, which includes both exercise and nutrition coaching.

2. What are two variations of that service and how do those variations relate to the core service?

Two components of Complete Online Fitness Coaching will be exercise and nutrition coaching. These can each be turned into a standalone service and then offered as the original, combined service. Both exercise and nutrition coaching are components of the core service that can be broken down into separate services to meet the demands and goals of clients.

3. What is one unique feature for each of those new variations?

Clients now have programs that are customized to focus on areas of their health that they feel they need the most support from a trainer on, instead of a one-size-fits-all package.

---

### Canned, Predesigned Programs

Chapter 2Building a BusinessLesson 2Building a Virtual Coaching Business Plan: Part ICanned, Predesigned ProgramsMany online personal trainers seek to write predesigned exercise and nutrition programs and sell them as one-off purchases. This approach may not be the most sustainable path for all businesses to take. Just like working with an in-person training client, training programs should be customized for each person. A training program that works for a 28-year-old former college athlete who wants to focus on muscle hypertrophy most likely will not work for a 65-year-old who wants to improve their ability to carry grocery bags up the stairs.
Figure Customize Predesigned Training TemplatesConsider the customer lifetime value (CLV). A customer who makes a recurring monthly payment for customized training at $100 per month for 4 months is worth 16 times more than someone who pays $25 for a one-time exercise program. They are also more likely to refer clients back to the business versus the templated training program that can be e-mailed to a friend.

This is not to say templates do not have a place in business. Templates can be massive time savers for the busy fitness professional and can make processes and work more efficient and consistent. Having pre-made training templates that are easy for the business owner to modify and customize for each client will save hours of work developing programming for each client (Figure: Customize Predesigned Training Templates). While similar, this differs from using a generic approach to programming for every client. For instance, developing five training templates with no specific exercises can save time having to recreate the same thing for every client.
Here are some template examples:


    Template 1: A 2-day-per-week full-body program
    Template 2: A 3-day-per-week program broken down into push/pull/legs
    Template 3: A 5-day-per-week program consisting of back/biceps, shoulders/chest/triceps, and legs

Lastly, consider the customer acquisition cost (CAC). New customers are not free—they cost time, money, and energy. The business may not have a marketing budget, but the time that is spent making sales and creating content is time away from running the business and time away from providing services to clients. It is generally more cost effective to sell to someone who is already a customer than to try and acquire a new customer.

---

### Live One-on-One Coaching

Chapter 2Building a BusinessLesson 2Building a Virtual Coaching Business Plan: Part ILive One-on-One CoachingNow that the three product offerings have been developed in nutrition, exercise/training, and nutrition and exercise coaching combined, it is time to build further on these products and provide some additional options to future clients. It is important to have valuable products for upsell to a client that they will benefit from. This will not only help clients increase their overall success but will also help boost the company's revenue as the CLV increases with each add-on. A simple option for this is to offer live video coaching for clients (Figure: Live Video Coaching). This is especially useful when the fitness professional is starting out and does not have a large client base. It enables the fitness professional to spend more time per client and obtain direct and instant feedback, and it provides more income per client for the business owner while they grow their business.
Continuing with the example company, three product offerings have been developed:


    Online Exercise Coaching
    Online Nutrition Coaching
    Complete Online Fitness Coaching

FigureLive Video CoachingA simple add-on for each of these products is a single-session video consultation. For a nutrition client, this could include a video call with the client while they go grocery shopping, being right there with them while they navigate the grocery store and work to make healthier food choices. For an exercise training client, this could include form checks or navigating the gym for a client who is nervous about going alone. Another option for add-ons is an in-person session. If the business owner has an online client who is local to their area, they could train with them at their gym, home, or outside (depending on their training/goals). For the nutrition client, they could actually go grocery shopping with them or help them learn how to meal prep. Adding these on to the product offerings, there is now a product line (Table: Upsell Options and Add-Ons).
Upsell Options and Add-OnsOnline Exercise Coaching
Online Nutrition Coaching
Online Exercise and Nutrition Coaching
Upsell option: Fitness and Nutrition Coaching
Upsell option: Fitness and Nutrition Coaching
Upsell option: N/A
Add-on option: Additional, 30-minute live video consult
Add-on option: Additional, 30-minute live video consult
Add-on option: Additional, 30-minute live video consult
With multiple coaching options, the chances have increased to upsell products to clients who will benefit from them, offer premium add-ons for existing clients, and keep the product overload to a minimum. The final step is pricing services and add-ons.

---

### Hybrid Model

Chapter 2Building a BusinessLesson 2Building a Virtual Coaching Business Plan: Part IHybrid ModelThe fitness professional also has the opportunity to build out their virtual coaching business with in-person clients, as well. This can allow the fitness professional to retain clients longer, take on a greater client load, and build their own online business while still working as an in-person trainer.
Options for this include the following:


    Working with nutrition clients online
    Intermediate- to advanced-level training clients who may not need direct supervision of a personal trainer but would like accountability and program design
    Utilizing client referrals for friends and family who may not be local to the training facility

Coach's CornerThe entrepreneur should keep their focus on their primary goal—their virtual coaching business. While the financial and employment stability that may come with an existing in-person training career is comfortable, the entrepreneur should ensure their attention is set on growing their online business. One of the many benefits of virtual coaching is the ability to take on more clients compared to in-person training. Spending valuable time with in-person training clients not only limits the personal trainer’s earning capability, but it can greatly slow down the rate of growth for the virtual business. The entrepreneur should find a balance between the two that steadily shifts focus toward the virtual coaching business.

---

### Market and Competitive Analysis

Chapter 2Building a BusinessLesson 2Building a Virtual Coaching Business Plan: Part IMarket and Competitive AnalysisFitness is an extremely competitive space, with almost 650,000 Americans employed by fitness or recreation centers and an expected growth rate of approximately 15% between 2018 to 2028 (Torpey, 2020; U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics, 2019). There is no difficulty in finding competitors in the fitness industry, no matter what niche a business targets. Analyzing the competitors in a market can be a daunting task; however, using a few key metrics (e.g., number of competitors in the market, average price of services, and the number of service variations offered) and incorporating a simplistic approach to designing pricing models and a target market, the fitness professional can save time and produce a quality business plan that will accelerate their growth by avoiding costly mistakes.
Rawpixel.com/Shutterstock.comWhen pricing any product, it is important to already know who the target market will be, what features will be offered with a well-defined product, and what variations of the product will be created. Once the fitness professional has these determined, they can take a look into the pricing of their competitors and accurately price themselves for the market. If the price is too low, there will not be enough revenue to pay the business owner a reasonable salary for work they have performed. If the price is too high, the business owner runs the risk of not having enough clients to pay themselves at all, or worse, they will have no clients. Many strategies exist for setting pricing for a new product. The most relevant and practical strategies will be broken down, including their benefits and downsides, and eventually one will be chosen for the example company. Before picking a pricing strategy, the fitness professional should conduct market research of future competitors in the area.
A simple online search for terms related to the target market and online coaching will produce ample results. Using the example company, a search for “online fitness coaching busy professionals” produced five quality results on the first page of the search engine. This provided pricing for five companies that offer online monthly coaching as follows in Table: Market Research Results Example.
Market Research Results ExampleCompany

The average price for monthly online coaching with these five companies is $243.

All of these companies offer similar services, each having their own unique USP. After this competitive analysis, it is known the average price in the market for online coaching is $243 per month and that the range in pricing for similar services is wide.
