### Internet Service Requirements

Chapter 3Technology Considerations Lesson 2Software RequirementsInternet Service Requirements The nature of virtual coaching relies on the ability to access the Internet with a reliable connection. Slow or inconsistent connections lead to frustrations for both the client and the fitness professional and can impact quality of service. With many Internet service providers available and variables in delivery of Internet service (fiber optic, cable, or satellite), it can be difficult to choose what is most effective for running a reliable virtual coaching business. The largest determining factor will be what is available in the area where the virtual coaching business is located and how it operates. If the fitness professional lives in a rural area, there might be limits to Internet services such as satellite or dial-up. If the fitness professional is in an urban area, likely there will be access to high-speed cable or fiber optic Internet services with high rates of bandwidth.
Bandwidth is the maximum data transfer rate of a network or Internet connection, in a specific amount of time, or how fast a person can download or upload information to and from the Internet. Bandwidth is measured in megabytes per second or Mbps. A Megabyte (Mb) is broken up into smaller units known as Kilobytes (kbit) and there are 1,000 Kilobytes in each Megabyte of data. Kilobytes are broken up into bits and there are 1000 bits per kilobyte. There is also a Gigabyte, which is 1,000 megabytes (Mb).
BandwidthThe maximum data transfer rate of a network or Internet connection, in a specific amount of time, or how fast Information can be downloaded or uploaded to and from the Internet.
MegabyteA measurement of data, 1,000 kilobytes.
KilobyteA measurement of data, 1,000 bits.

---

### Content

Chapter 3Technology Considerations Lesson 2Software RequirementsThere are some basic considerations for how much bandwidth is needed to operate a virtual coaching business. If providing online group training via a video conferencing platform where most of the data transfer comes from, slower Internet speeds may be utilized without an effect on the user experience. For example, Zoom, Skype, and Google Meet require as little as 1.5 Mbps for voice or low-definition video calls and up to 8 Mbps for large group video conferencing or HD video calling. A 25 Mbps Internet speed may be enough in this case.
If creating video content, performing large live stream feeds, or regularly downloading and uploading large files is integral to how the virtual coaching business is run, getting a higher bandwidth connection can increase productivity. For live-feed video conferencing, a slower Internet speed may impact the video feed quality and end-user experience (Table: Comparing Bandwidth Speed and Download Times).

### Content

Chapter 3Technology Considerations Lesson 2Software RequirementsDial-up will have the slowest rates of bandwidth at just 56 kbits or 0.056 Mbps. When compared to the U.S. average broadband speed of around 135 Mbps, dial up is over 2,000 times slower. Just 0.3% of the U.S. is still using dial-up Internet. This is just enough to do basic tasks like answer e-mails and read webpages on the Internet at a slow pace. While dial-up is still available in some rural places, this Internet connection would not be suitable for running a virtual coaching business.
Satellite Internet is used by 6.9% of the U.S. population (Clement, 2019) and has a limitation of around 100 Mbps, with most services capping around 25Mbps. While this is enough to run a virtual coaching business, satellite Internet may be more expensive and less reliable than a cable or fiber Internet connection. Satellite Internet also operates on the line-of-sight principle. Meaning the satellites must be able to physically see each other to form a connection. A simple storm with poor weather conditions can knock out the Internet connection due to the satellites not being able to connect. This can make running a business a hassle if the connection to the Internet is unreliable. Additionally, satellite Internet tends to be more expensive.
Broadband Internet connections such as DSL, fiber optics, and cable Internet will be the fastest, most reliable, and often cheapest Internet connections to choose from. With bandwidth speeds from 25 Mbps to 1,000+ Mbps and reliable connections that do not rely on line-of-sight, a virtual coaching business can operate efficiently and reliably.

In order to be effective, efficient, and productive, consider signing up with an Internet service provider that can provide 25 to 75 Mbps of bandwidth. This will ensure smooth operation when uploading training videos, administering virtual coaching sessions, or performing video consultations.
Try ThisTry searching for “Internet Speed Test,” and then test your current Internet speed. If it is running less than 25 Mbps, it may be time for you to upgrade your Internet package or Internet service provider.

---

### Computer Peripheral Requirements

Chapter 3Technology Considerations Lesson 3Computer Peripheral RequirementsComputer Peripheral Requirements Andrii Zastrozhnov/Shutterstock.comBy now, the fitness professional should be able to determine what hardware and software requirements are needed, how much bandwidth is needed to run a business, and what operating systems are preferred when purchasing a computer. One last consideration is computer peripherals, which may help with producing and delivering quality content to the client. The fitness professional should remember that these additional peripherals are not requirements but, rather, components that contribute to producing and delivering higher-quality content. A fitness professional may be able to start and operate a virtual coaching business solely using a smartphone and then upgrade as their budget allows.

Computer peripherals include the following:


    Lighting
    Cameras
    Microphones

 



LightingA major consideration for creating high-quality, deliverable digital content is lighting. Lighting may impact overall content quality, considering that not all training facilities or office spaces will have adequate lighting. A lack of bright natural light when filming technique videos, live workouts, or virtual coaching sessions can be a major distraction. The fitness professional should mitigate this with a well-lit environment.

The main factors to consider when purchasing lighting peripherals are:


    Lighting Spectrum
    Budget and Portability

---

### Lighting Spectrum

Chapter 3Technology Considerations Lesson 3Computer Peripheral RequirementsLighting Spectrum Lighting spectrum or color temperature is what hue of color is being provided by a light source. Degrees of Kelvin (K) is how the appearance of light is measured. Kelvin is measured from 1,000 K up to 10,000 K. The lower end of the Kelvin spectrum has a far-red tinge to it. The high end of the Kelvin spectrum has a blue overtone.
The typical lighting spectrums used in video content is between 3,200 K and 5,500 K. The 3,200 K lighting spectrum is typically associated with indoor lighting and indoor scenes; it will be warmer in color with a higher degree of orange or yellow. The 5,500 K spectrum is a cooler light that is mostly bluish-white and is representative of midday daylight. Too low of a lighting spectrum will produce more flat colors and may create unnatural skin tone, while too high of a lighting spectrum will lack warmth and be harsh to look at.
The 5,600 K spectrum may be more optimal for use where there is a lower level of light or in indoor settings where incandescent bulbs or soft, warm lighting may be used. The midday lighting spectrum will help produce a brighter, more natural and balanced feel. The warmer 3,200 K spectrum may be more optimal for use where there is harsh white light such as under fluorescent bulbs or when filming in brightly lit locations. The warmer spectrum may reduce the harsh look that higher spectrum lighting may produce.
Many of the available lighting options will have adjustability from 3,200 K to 5,600 K, which means the fitness professional will be able to adjust the lighting spectrum accordingly for where they will be filming the majority of their content. Having this adjustability can minimize purchasing extra equipment or lighting options.

### Budget and Portability

Chapter 3Technology Considerations Lesson 3Computer Peripheral RequirementsBudget and PortabilityProducing high-quality content that is valuable to the client does not require purchasing high-end equipment. While there is studio quality lighting available at a steep price, a fitness professional just starting out who is looking to film training videos and perform virtual coaching sessions will not need such high-end equipment. Building a virtual coaching business also means building a business that is portable. With much of the technology to run a business being available on a smartphone, tablet, or laptop, working on the go will present some added challenges if it means having to carry around bulky equipment.
With budget and portability in mind, the fitness professional may find appropriate solutions in portable ring lights or clip-on smartphone lights that are both compact and effective in meeting lighting requirements and lighting spectrum applications. The ring lights are typically adjustable (Figure: Ring Light), while the clip-on lights may not be (Figure: Clip-On Light). However, these lights can work in the gym when filming training content, at home when performing video consultation calls, or when administering virtual coaching sessions to the client.
Some free or low-budget solutions to lighting might be as simple as opting to film content in a(n):


    Brighter location on sunny days
    Office that has a large amount of natural daylight
    Room utilizing a lamp fixture with a cool white bulb installed and the lampshade removed


While these options may be less-optimal, they can help the fitness professional create higher-quality content without investing too much into equipment.
Genzi/Shutterstock.comFigureRing LightVetertravel/Shutterstock.comFigureClip-On Light

---

### Camera

Chapter 3Technology Considerations Lesson 3Computer Peripheral RequirementsCameraOne struggle that the fitness professional may encounter when building a virtual coaching business is that it lacks the same face-to-face contact as in-person fitness coaching. With over 50% of human communication conveyed through body language (Park & Park, 2018), being able to film quality workout videos, take photos for demonstrations, perform video consultation calls, and have regular video check-ins may provide higher client engagement.
The considerations when looking for a camera include the following:


    Megapixel (MP) rating of the camera
    Video recording resolution
    Budget

Megapixel Rating While a bit of data is the smallest portion of data that a computer processes, a pixel is the smallest portion of an image. They are tiny little dots or squares of color that, when put together, make up an image. A digital image may look similar to a regular image but when zoomed in close enough, a digital image is made up of millions of these tiny squares. A megapixel is 1 million pixels, and the more megapixels in an image, the more detail and clarity an image will have. Notice that in Figure: Close-Up of Pixels, each little dot is actually a little square of color.
Pikepicture/Shutterstock.comFigureClose-Up of PixelsThe megapixel rating of a camera will have a determinant effect on the quality of the photos being taken. While there is no set recommendation for how many megapixels are needed for creating content, generally speaking, a camera with a higher number of megapixels will take higher-quality photos than a camera with a lower megapixel rating. The fitness professional should consider purchasing a camera with the highest megapixel rating that is within their budget.
PixelThe smallest portion of an image. They are tiny little dots or squares of color that, when put together, make up an image.
Megapixel1 million pixels put together, a measure of image resolution or graphic resolution.

---

### Video Resolution

Chapter 3Technology Considerations Lesson 3Computer Peripheral RequirementsVideo ResolutionVideo resolution is another representation of image quality. The higher the video resolution, the higher the video quality. Much like an image is made from pixels, video resolution relates to how many pixels the video image has both in horizontal count and vertical count. 720p, 1080p, and 4K ultra HD are the most common resolutions for video content. Most modern digital cameras will have at minimum 1080p full HD video resolution capabilities and that is really all that would be needed for filming video content, conducting virtual coaching sessions, or performing video consultation calls. Table: Comparing Different Video Resolutions explains these different video resolutions.

### Budget and Peripherals

Chapter 3Technology Considerations Lesson 3Computer Peripheral RequirementsBudget and Peripherals Budget is the final consideration when purchasing a camera for a virtual coaching business. There are a few camera styles to look at and the fitness professional should do their own research to determine what is best for their needs and what is available within their price range.


    DSLR camera: Professional quality photos, typically films video in HD, higher price point, and may need to buy attachments
    Hand-held digital camera: Lower-quality but hand-held, no additional lenses needed, and reasonably priced
    Smartphone camera: Newer phones will have all of the features needed to produce high-quality video and images

Getting TechnicalThe iPhone has had HD 1,080 p recording capabilities since the iPhone 5 was released in late 2012, and Samsung, with their S4 in spring of 2013, also has 1,080 p HD recording capabilities. Earlier phones still had a respectable 8-megapixel rating and 13-megapixel rating for the S4. However, these earlier smartphones may not have some functionality like changing specific settings or auto-focus that newer smartphones may have. While there is no standard recommendation for megapixel requirements for high-quality photos, most smartphones will have a 10+ megapixel camera. If the fitness professional has a relatively new smartphone, they will be able to shoot, record, edit, and upload much of their content right from the smartphone itself. 
One upgrade to consider when using a camera for recording content or even taking photographs is to purchase a quality tripod. Options vary on this due to the variety of brands and models of cameras and smartphones. It will be up to the fitness professional to research what will work best with their camera or smartphone.
In addition to capturing quality content, most laptops will have a built-in webcam. Most newer computers will have a webcam with at least 720p HD resolution for filming. While 720p is considered a lower-quality resolution for filming, a camera with a 720p resolution is still usable to get started with filming content for a virtual coaching business. There are also a multitude of USB webcam options available that will plug into the fitness professional’s computer system and may have higher resolution ratings. A webcam is a lower-budget option that can easily improve content quality without a large initial expense.

---

### Audio

Chapter 3Technology Considerations Lesson 3Computer Peripheral RequirementsAudioWhen filming or recording high-quality content for a virtual coaching business, fitness professionals should ensure that the sound quality of the content being produced is considered. Poor audio quality or videos with excessive background noise may lead to the client retaining less of the information and impair learning and communication (Klatte et al., 2013). This may lead to added struggles and create a discord between the client and the fitness professional. Purchasing a microphone can help create a quality audio feed when recording content such as podcasts, training videos, and voice-overs, and when performing virtual coaching sessions.



There are two main types of microphones to consider:


    Dynamic Microphones
    Condenser Microphones


Dynamic microphones are the types of microphones one might see being used by a singer or comedian performing on stage (Figure: Dynamic Microphone). These microphones tend to do better than condenser microphones in environments that have a higher level of background noises. That is why dynamic microphones are used in concerts, during stand-up shows, or interviewing somebody on the street. They contain a coil and magnet inside that are sensitive to sound vibrations. Dynamic microphones are also more rugged than condenser microphones, so, if the microphone gets dropped, there may be less worry of damage and having to replace an expensive peripheral. These microphones are relatively cheap compared to condenser microphones and require no extra equipment. They can sit at a desk in the quiet to be used for podcast recordings or used in a noisy environment such as filming workout videos.
Soonios Pro/Stuttershock.comFigureDynamic MicrophoneKrakenimages.com/Shutterstock.comFigureCondenser MicrophoneCondenser microphones work slightly differently than their dynamic counterpart (Figure: Condenser Microphone). Condenser microphones do not contain a coil and magnet and instead use capacitor plates, which are more sensitive to the smaller sound vibrations than a dynamic microphone. This can help pick up the subtle differences in a voice and may be better for recording a podcast in a quiet area. Condenser microphones are also not as durable as a dynamic microphone due to their construction using capacitor plates. Condenser microphones may also need an external power source or internal battery to operate. Depending on the type of microphone purchased may mean hidden equipment costs such as extra audio equipment. However, condenser microphones that are powered by battery or USB plug-in are becoming more available making these microphones a viable option for fitness professionals without added costs.

---

### Content

Chapter 3Technology Considerations Lesson 3Computer Peripheral RequirementsThe next consideration for microphones is whether to choose a USB microphone or an XLR microphone. Simply put, a USB microphone will plug right into the computer, an XLR microphone will need an audio interface between the computer and the microphone (Figure: USB Microphone). A USB microphone is simple and considered plug and play, with no additional software, drivers, or expensive audio interface systems. XLR microphones can become more expensive due to both initial purchase of the microphone and required add-ons needed to operate (Figure: XLR Microphone). However, XLR microphones (dynamic or condenser) will produce a higher-quality sound than a USB microphone.
Random Photo Gallery/Shutterstock.comFigureUSB MicrophoneSockagphoto/Shutterstock.comFigureXLR MicrophoneOne final option is to simply use a smartphone with a voice recording app. Fitness professionals should be mindful that this option will not produce the highest-quality audio recordings. Most smartphones come with a voice recording app that may have adjustable settings. There are also third-party apps that may increase recording sound quality. The fitness professional should research what may be best for this option.

If the fitness professional is also filming live workouts or doing large group instruction over video conference software, purchasing an additional wearable microphone may be needed and can improve audio quality and engagement with the clients (Figure: Wearable Microphone).

The Table: Dynamic Microphone Versus Condenser Microphone compares and contrasts the pros and cons of microphones. The fitness professional can use this as a guide before purchasing a microphone.
Di Studio/Shutterstock.comFigureWearable MicrophoneDynamic Microphone Versus Condenser MicrophoneDynamic Microphone
Condenser Microphone
Budget: When considering budget, how the microphone connects is important. USB will be plug and play, whereas XLR microphones need an interface to record.
A dynamic microphone will typically be cheaper than a condenser microphone. Using a dynamic microphone with a USB connection will be budget friendly and plug and play with no audio interface needed.
Condenser microphones are generally more expensive than a dynamic microphone. A condenser microphone that uses an XLR connection may be expensive due to initial costs because of the added audio interface that is required for XLR microphones.
Environment Considerations: Where is the audio being recorded?
A dynamic microphone will be better suited for noisy environments.
A condenser microphone will be better suited for quiet environments.
Durability: Will the fitness professional be travelling around?
A dynamic microphone is more rugged than a condenser microphone and may be better suited to the more-mobile fitness professional.
A condenser is well-suited for the less-mobile fitness professional.

### Fitness Programming Software Options

Chapter 3Technology Considerations Lesson 4Fitness Programming Software OptionsFitness Programming Software Options There are a multitude of fitness, nutrition, and workout apps available for both the client and the fitness professional to utilize in reaching their health and fitness goals. Studies have shown that people may have greater adherence to their health goals when using mobile health apps to self-monitor and stay accountable (Voth et al., 2016). While no single app, no matter how good it is, can replace a qualified and effective fitness professional, using these apps may increase a client’s engagement and adherence to their program.
ZinetroN/Shutterstock.comNutrition Apps While tracking nutritional intake has numerous benefits, many of the new apps do much more than simply count calories. This can be especially important for those who are trying to lose weight, build muscle, or improve athletic performance. Nutritional apps are beneficial for the following reasons:


    Meal planning
    Understanding and counting calories
    Reading and understanding food labels
    Tracking macronutrient intake (protein, carbohydrates, and fat)
    Tracking nutritional content (vitamins, minerals, and fiber)
    Making healthier food choices
    Fueling workouts
    Finding recipes
    Forming positive eating habits

Coach’s CornerThe fitness professional should remember that when recommending that clients track or monitor meals away from home or at restaurants, there can be an 8 to 18% difference in what is indicated on the label versus the actual calories being eaten, and, in some cases, there can be up to 200% more calories. (Urban et al., 2010) All it takes is a heavy-handed chef to add a little extra oil or sauce to a dish to increase the calorie value of a meal. It may be a good idea to have the client over-estimate meals out by 30% and also account for an additional 100 to 200 calories worth of fat from oil or butters being used. The fitness professional and client should work together to help estimate the calorie content of food.

---

### Fitness Apps

Chapter 3Technology Considerations Lesson 4Fitness Programming Software OptionsFitness Apps Fitness apps are downloadable applications that can be used on nearly any mobile device and are designed to help users obtain certain fitness goals. A fitness app is an inexpensive and convenient way for fitness enthusiasts to take their workouts with them and include a variety of exercise formats, including high-intensity interval training (HIIT), strength training, yoga, and Pilates.
The benefits of fitness apps include the following:


    Access to exercise routines anytime or anywhere with a computer, tablet, or smartphone
    Personalized fitness programs based on the user’s goals and exercise preferences
    Access to exercise programs that do not require equipment
    Tracking completed workouts and time spent exercising
    Daily/weekly/monthly reminders to complete workout routines
    Simulation of one-on-one or group training
    Tracking steps taken each day
    Tracking calories expended from physical activity
    Tracking fitness achievements like miles run or weight lifted
    Instructing proper exercise technique

There are several fitness apps on the market today and many have free trial periods. Individuals can test several applications to determine which app best fits their needs. Remember that individuals with chronic disease may need medical clearance before starting a new exercise routine. In addition, performing exercise routines without supervision can be dangerous, especially for those new to exercise.
NASM EDGE App NASM has their own app for fitness professionals called NASM EDGE (Figure: NASM EDGE 1 & 2).

The NASM EDGE app has the following features:


    Exercise Video Library: Assess and share the hundreds of NASM expert-approved exercises.
    Calorie and Macronutrient Calculator: Give clients confidence with nutrition advice that aligns with their goals.
    Workout Builder: Create customized workouts or use ready-to-go training sessions designed by NASM experts.
    Integrated Client Dashboard: Keep track of clients and monitor their real-time performance, compare dynamic assessments, and send clients workouts.
    Assessment Wizard: Remove any uncertainty out of client assessments while performing them quickly and effectively.


The app also has a built-in Certified Personal Trainer (CPT) prep tool for those studying for their NASM-CPT examination. The prep tool includes over 600 practice questions, over 500 flash cards, and an unlimited number of attempts on the NASM practice exam. 
FigureNASM EDGE 1FigureNASM EDGE 2

---

### Wearables

Chapter 3Technology Considerations Lesson 4Fitness Programming Software OptionsWearables Wearable fitness technology has been around as early as 1981, when Olympic medalists were using wearable heart rate monitors. Commercial gyms started seeing wireless heart rate monitors in the early 2000s being integrated into group workout classes. Since 2010, when Fitbit released the first clip-on pedometer, technology has gone from basic step counting to tracking heart rate, sleep, energy expenditure, and even GPS tracking.
Clients can track virtually every meaningful data point about themselves, which can be helpful but can also provide additional confusion in an already overwhelming abundance of information. Keep in mind, the current body of evidence is mixed regarding the weight-loss and health benefits from wearable fitness technology (Jo et al., 2019). While the direct benefits for the client may still be in question from a research perspective, there are valuable metrics that can be tracked such as heart rate, sleep, and energy expenditure. The fitness professional can use this information to help customize and evaluate exercise programs. When considering what is best for a client, note that some of the metrics being tracked by wearable technology will be more accurate than others.
Heart Rate wavebreakmedia/Shutterstock.comThere are multiple studies showing that heart rate monitoring will be most accurate at rest or at moderate activity levels, with accuracy decreasing as exercise intensity increases (Khushhal et al., 2017). If a client is doing higher-intensity or long-duration cardiovascular training such as cycling or running, a better option to recommend is a fitness tracker that comes with a wearable heart rate chest strap (Reddy et al., 2018). If a client is looking for general heart rate monitoring, a wristband style fitness tracker will suffice.
Energy Expenditure Many fitness trackers will estimate total daily energy expenditure (TDEE) and provide the end user with a calorie burn count at the end of each day. How accurate is this measure of TDEE? Studies show that most wearable fitness trackers have a range of accuracy and, much like heart rate, as exercise intensity increases, measured energy expenditure accuracy may decrease (Kendall et al., 2019). When compared to research-grade fitness trackers, resting calorie expenditure may be close. However, these devices tend to overestimate time spent in higher-intensity activities, which leads to higher-than-actual energy expenditures being reported by the wearables (Feehan et al., 2018). This simply means that current wearables may do well at calculating energy expenditure at rest, but the level of accuracy decreases as exercise intensity increases. One study showed a 20 to 50% difference in actual calories expended versus what the wearables listed when recording energy expenditure during resistance training (Bai et al., 2016). However, it is important to note that the quality of the device’s electronics and algorithms have a fundamental influence on the accuracy of the device. In other words, some wearables are more accurate than others. Due to the high degree of inconsistency across wearable fitness trackers, fitness professionals should use caution when relying on this information.
Energy expenditureThe number of calories burned over a specified period of time at a given intensity of movement.
Total daily energy expenditure (TDEE)The number of calories a person burns over a 24-hr period.

---

### Step Counters/Pedometers

Chapter 3Technology Considerations Lesson 4Fitness Programming Software OptionsStep Counters/Pedometers Most step counters are fairly accurate when it comes to measuring stride length and accurately representing step counts, with an average error of 3 to 6% on some brands (Feehan et al., 2018). There are also multiple studies that show an improvement in all-cause mortality that is associated with higher step counts (Dwyer et al., 2015). In addition to the health benefits of a higher step count, monitoring step counts can help a fitness professional monitor for a subconscious decrease in Non-Exercise Activity Thermogenesis (NEAT) that is often associated with weight-loss-based goals among clients (Trexler et al., 2014).
Kaspars Grinvalds/Shutterstock.comNEAT includes all movements and activities of daily living that are not structured exercise such as fidgeting, performing household chores, grooming, and pacing. If a client starts out at a 10,000-per-day step count average at the start of a weight-loss program and through their program ends up subconsciously reducing step counts to 7,000 per day, there may be a notable difference in TDEE. By monitoring step counts, clients can be encouraged to move more and avoid this downward metabolic adaptation that occurs while working to achieve a fat-loss-related goal. This is where wearable fitness technology excels in terms of accuracy, alongside being the most usable data for the fitness professional.
Coach’s CornerThe fitness professional should always try to meet the client where they are at when it comes to step counts. Assigning a 10,000-per-day step count to a client who is only achieving 2,000 steps per day may feel overwhelming to the client. Try to encourage the client to move more where they can while helping them understand NEAT can impact daily energy expenditure. Set small goals, even just 5 to 10 minutes of extra movement a day may contribute greatly to overall step counts over a week.
SleepOne very popular function of many fitness trackers that has evolved over time is the ability to monitor sleep. Sleep impacts appetite (Brondel et al., 2010) and hunger and can affect the client's rate of recovery from their training sessions. Most fitness trackers have sleep monitors that are shown to be relatively accurate when compared to self-reported sleep time (Lee et al., 2018).
Andrey_Popov/Shutterstock.comWhile the sleep trackers on wearable fitness technology may not be on par with the technology used to monitor sleep in clinical research studies, for the client and the fitness professional, the accuracy of sleep tracking in wearable fitness technology will suffice in assessing sleep hygiene. Monitoring a client's sleeping patterns may help the client to improve sleep hygiene and overall sleep quality.
Food for Thought6 hours and 40 minutes is the average amount of sleep an American gets (Sleep Foundation, 2014).
Non-exercise activity thermogenesis (NEAT)The measurement of energy expenditure from all other movements outside of intentional exercise activity.

---

### Integrating Apps and Wearables Into Business

Chapter 3Technology Considerations Lesson 4Fitness Programming Software OptionsIntegrating Apps and Wearables Into Business Whether using the NASM Edge App, third-party client management software, or any of the other apps listed, it is important for the fitness professional to understand how to best use these integrations when operating a business. Remember that apps and software programs have their advantages and disadvantages and there is no one correct approach for building a virtual coaching business. Budget, business scaling, client population, and ease of client use all play a role in determining which programs and apps to use. For example, when getting started, a fitness professional may choose to simply use spreadsheets for workouts and nutrition tracking. This simple system can be a low-cost solution for the fitness professional and can help their clients build effective health habits. Over time, integrating wearables such as a pedometer or activity tracker can help the client more accurately track their physical activity and will add additional value.
If a fitness professional is working with a higher client load or scaling a business and building a team of coaches, using an all-in-one client management software program may lead to a more scalable and productive approach. All-in-one client management software systems may incorporate the functions a fitness professional may need:


    Billing and invoicing systems
    Client reminder and check-in systems
    Brand customization
    Nutrition tracking
    Syncing with wearable fitness to track activity
    Workout videos and exercise programming tools

Even if the fitness professional is not operating with a high volume of clients, many of the benefits that client management software systems offer will help a small-scale online business run smoothly and productively.
The software and app options are major considerations for creating the foundations of client management and a professionally delivered coaching experience in a virtual coaching business. Each client management software system has their own unique features; therefore, it is important to conduct further research and consider all aspects that will be needed for operating a virtual coaching business.
While these options and recommendations have the fitness professional in mind, it is also important to consider the client when using these different approaches. The less-complicated the system being used, the lower the barrier of effort it will be for the client. This lower barrier of effort for coaching interaction means a client can focus on the coaching itself rather than learning a complicated system. For some, filling out spreadsheets may be an effortless portion of their check-ins, but for others it may be a barrier of effort that causes additional struggles in adherence to the coaching program. Using a simple system such as a client management software program may allow the fitness professional to reduce time spent performing other tasks and give their clients a higher level of service. It also may reduce these critical barriers of effort for the client.

---

### Summary

Chapter 3Technology Considerations Lesson 4Fitness Programming Software OptionsSummaryThis chapter outlined what hardware, software, and peripherals are needed to deliver a highly professional and individualized coaching experience to the client.

Fitness professionals who offer virtual coaching must become knowledgeable about computer hardware and software systems. These systems include computer processing and storage, operating software, lighting, and cameras.
Fitness professionals must also acquire basic knowledge of fitness programming software, including nutrition and fitness apps and wearable technology.
Technology considerations when building a virtual coaching business are an important part of delivering a high-quality and personalized service to a client. With technology becoming more prevalent within the fitness industry, the fitness professional who can adapt and grow with these ever-changing technologies can build a successful and sustainable virtual coaching business. Please refer to the video below for a summary of the topics discussed within this chapter.
