# Chapter 7: Integrating Wearables Into Virtual Coaching

### Chapter 7: Integrating Wearables Into Virtual Coaching

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual CoachingLesson 1Introduction to Fitness WearablesChapter 7: Integrating Wearables Into Virtual Coaching Wearable technology unlocks the ability to incorporate objective biometrics related to daily activity, sleep, recovery, and health parameters to develop and dynamically adjust integrative training programs. This chapter will focus on education and validation of health and performance for common wearable biometrics, the development of data-driven programs, and how to leverage wearable data for compliance monitoring and client reinforcement to maintain motivation and retention.
Learning ObjectivesAfter completing this chapter, the NASM Virtual Coach will have the ability to do the following:


    Develop an understanding of wearable technology and its limitations in virtual training.
    Summarize the potential value of integrating wearable devices for virtual training.
    Describe the primary biometrics collected by commercial wearable devices.
    Determine ways to use biometric data to develop individualized programs.
    Define ways to make data-driven adjustments to optimize safety, outcomes, and compliance.

---

### Introduction

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual CoachingLesson 1Introduction to Fitness WearablesIntroduction According to Vogels (2020), “Roughly one-in-five U.S. adults (21%) say they regularly wear a smartwatch or wearable fitness tracker” (Figure: One-In-Five). When available, wearable technology provides fitness professionals with the ability to improve the reliability of data collection and continuously monitor clients’ health and fitness biometrics in a virtual training environment. The data from wearable devices can be utilized for assessing client risk and readiness for physical activity, developing individualized programs, monitoring progress, and even as a reinforcement tool to optimize adherence and retention.
 ra2 studio/Shutterstock.comFigureOne-In-FiveBiometricsMeasurements of the human body or characteristics, including those obtained from smartwatches and wearable devices.

---

### Defining Wearables

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual CoachingLesson 1Introduction to Fitness WearablesDefining WearablesWith the use of wearables, the virtual coaching experience does not have to be limited to an initial assessment, intermittent communication, and subjective reporting. When integrated properly, wearable technology helps to facilitate a 24/7 monitoring approach. This approach can improve client outcomes while also optimizing a fitness professional’s ability to scale his or her virtual coaching business.
While the perception of wearable technology is often limited to wrist-worn devices, this industry includes any type of electronic device worn on the body. Other common wearable devices include smart jewelry, tech-enabled clothing, and eyewear. For the purposes of virtual coaching, this chapter will limit its focus to commercially available smartwatches and fitness trackers that collect and track applicable biometrics including the following:


    Heart rate
    Heart rate variability
    Respiratory rate
    Oxygen saturation
    Physical activity
    Sleep parameters


To collect physiological data, smartwatches and fitness trackers often come equipped with sensors, including an electrocardiogram (ECG) or photoplethysmogram (PPG), gyroscope, and accelerometer.
Description of Wearable SensorsSensor Type
Description
Electrocardiogram (ECG)
Direct measurement of the heart’s electrical activity
Photoplethysmogram (PPG)
Optical measurement of blood circulation obtained by illuminating the skin and measuring changes in light absorption
Gyroscope
Measurement of orientation in space
Accelerometer
Measurement of three-axis movement and acceleration
WearableAny type of electronic technology worn on the device, including smartwatches, fitness trackers, smart jewelry and tech-enabled clothing.

---

### Differences in Wearable Devices

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual CoachingLesson 1Introduction to Fitness WearablesDifferences in Wearable DevicesPeter Kotoff/Shutterstock.comWearable technologies have many differences, including the type of embedded sensors, method of data collection, what they measure, advanced features, and the quality and reliability of the data. When integrating wearable devices into a virtual coaching business, fitness professionals should be able to adapt and work with a device already owned by a client, but also be prepared to make suggestions based on the most suitable option for their needs.
Getting TechnicalElectrocardiography (ECG) Versus Photoplethysmography (PPG) in Wearable Technology 

Electrocardiography (ECG) is a measurement of electrical activity at the heart that controls the expansion and contraction of the heart chambers, often using multiple electrodes. ECG is the gold standard measurement used in hospitals and healthcare settings to monitor heart rate and heart rate variability.

Photoplethysmography (PPG) uses light-based technology to measure the rate of blood flow as controlled by the heart’s pumping action. In addition to measurements of heart rate, analysis of the PPG waveform can provide an accurate estimation of additional biometrics, depending on the wavelength or color of light utilized.

FIGURE: ECG vs. PPG
FigureECG vs. PPG

---

### Green Versus Red Light PPG

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual CoachingLesson 1Introduction to Fitness WearablesGreen Versus Red Light PPG Most wearable devices utilize green light PPG. Measurements done on light-skinned individuals at normal temperature have shown that green light has desired properties for data collection, particularly during motion (Maeda et al., 2011). Green light PPG has been shown to be more readily absorbed by individuals with higher melanin levels (Shcherbina et al., 2017), including those with darker skin tones, which can often lead to inaccuracies in data collection.
Red and infrared (IR) light PPG sensors, while less reliable during physical activity, have various benefits related to deeper penetration in the skin (Figure: Green/Red Light PPG) and typically provide more reliable data regardless of skin tone (Spigulis, Gailite, Lihachev, & Erts, 2007). To correct for inaccuracies caused by motion in red light PPG sensors, many wearable technology companies have created algorithms that utilize the accelerometer to produce high-quality PPG signals. For these adaptive filters, the accelerometer movement data is used to detect and subtract motion from the PPG signal or to exclude the data completely if an accuracy threshold cannot be obtained.
FigureGreen/Red Light PPGAdvanced FeaturesThe features and biometric data collected from wearable devices will vary by manufacturer and even by device offered by the same wearable manufacturer. The number of advanced features often increases with the cost of the device, including color screens, onboard GPS, and waterproofing. More expensive is not always better, as many features may be unnecessary or lead to additional confusion for the client. Fitness professionals must understand what each device offers. They must know how the device relates to their ability to develop and execute effective virtual coaching and consider the balance between cost, features, and ease of use for the client.

---

### Data Reliability

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual CoachingLesson 1Introduction to Fitness WearablesData ReliabilityAndrey_Popov/Shutterstock.comWhen relied on for risk stratification, program development, and client monitoring, the accuracy of data collected is of the utmost importance. In a comparative study of six mainstream wearable fitness trackers, researchers found that “Fitness trackers of different brands vary with regard to the measurement of indicators and are all affected by the activity state” (Xie et al., 2018).
All wearable technologies will have some margin of error relative to costly and bulky medical equipment. However, using the same device consistently will allow the client to establish a baseline activity level and show the fitness professional a developing trend to support improvements and adjustments. In general, fitness professionals should select a reliable device, avoid switching between different devices, and focus on establishing and monitoring data trends over time.
Coach’s CornerSelecting a Reliable Wearable Technology

When determining which wearable technology to integrate into a virtual coaching business, fitness professionals should research which devices have been clinically validated versus gold standard medical equipment. For example, heart rate and/or oxygen saturation should be validated against a hospital-grade electrocardiogram or pulse oximeter and not against other commercial devices lacking validation.

Most commercial wearable technologies do not have the approval of the U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA approval) but may have a research section available on their company website. Additionally, fitness professionals may search for scholarly articles on Google Scholar, JSTOR, or a comparable resource for information about data reliability of certain devices.
Risk stratificationAssessment of risk based on qualifying information, such as vitals or health history – Risk stratification can include the potential for training-related injury, the likelihood of a health event, or developing a health condition.

---

### Pros and Cons

While wearable technology presents the opportunity to improve a virtual coaching experience, fitness professionals should not rely solely on biometric data. The trainer–client connection should include communication and consideration of subjective feelings and opinions, in addition to objective data obtained from wearable devices.

---

### Biometric Education

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual Coaching Lesson 2Biometric EducationBiometric EducationBiometrics are measurements of the human body or characteristics. These measurements range from fingerprint recognition to more readily available measurements obtained from smartwatches and fitness trackers. To understand how to integrate biometric data into a virtual coaching business, fitness professionals should first have an in-depth understanding of that data by considering these questions (Figure: Evaluating Biometrics).
FigureEvaluating Biometrics

---

### Heart Rate

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual Coaching Lesson 2Biometric EducationHeart RateBreslavtsev Oleg/Shutterstock.comHeart rate, measured in beats per minute (bpm), is the number of times the heart beats per minute. Heart rate is most commonly assessed at rest and during physical activity, both of which should be considered independently. According to the American Heart Association (2016), the following factors may influence heart rate:


    Fitness Level
    Room Temperature
    Body Position
    Emotional Stress
    Body Size or Composition
    Use of Medications

Critical!The Role of a Fitness Professional

A fitness professional is not credentialed to diagnose, treat, or prevent any specific medical conditions. While many of these biometrics may provide insight into potential health issues, the role of wearable technology in virtual coaching is to provide reliable data for program development. Fitness professionals should not provide medical advice but, rather, recommend that clients seek medical attention from a qualified health professional if alarming biometric values or trends are recognized, especially if accompanied by symptoms including chest pain, shortness of breath, dizziness, or fatigue.

---

### Resting Heart Rate

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual Coaching Lesson 2Biometric EducationResting Heart RateResting heart rate (RHR) is the number of beats per minute obtained during rest. A normal resting heart rate can vary widely from person to person, but the accepted range for adults is 60 to 100 bpm (American Heart Association, 2015) (Figure: Resting Heart Rate).
FigureResting Heart RateHowever, individuals with higher resting heart rate values within this range may still be at increased risk of cardiovascular, non-cardiovascular, and all-cause mortality (Seccareccia et al., 2001). An elevated resting heart rate above 100 bpm, referred to as tachycardia, can be indicative of an underlying health condition that should be addressed by a trained healthcare professional.
In general, a lower resting heart rate is related to improved heart function and cardiovascular fitness (Nauman et al., 2012). A well-trained individual may maintain a resting heart rate well below 60 bpm, referred to as bradycardia, due to positive cardiovascular adaptations from training. For clients who are not physically active but still maintain a heart rate below 60 bpm, fitness professionals should prompt the client to seek approval from a doctor prior to engaging in physical activity, as this can be a sign of an underlying heart condition (American Heart Association, 2016).
When assessed properly, resting heart rate should be relatively stable for all populations with minimal day-to-day changes. Over time, positive or negative lifestyle interventions may lead to a steady change in resting heart rate values. In a meta-analysis, Reimers et al. (2018) found that all types of exercise, especially endurance training and yoga, decreased resting heart rate in healthy subjects, which may contribute to a reduction in all-cause mortality.
Resting heart rate (RHR)The number of times the heart beats per minute while at complete rest.

---

### Active Heart Rate

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual Coaching Lesson 2Biometric EducationActive Heart RateActive heart rate is the number of beats per minute obtained during physical activity. In general, active heart rate is correlated with relative output and can be utilized to gauge the intensity of an exercise effort (Figure: Target Heart Rate). Fitness professionals may utilize active heart rate monitoring as a method to assess compliance for exercise prescription, particularly when target heart rate zones are recommended (Polar, 2020).
FigureTarget Heart RateMany wearable devices rely on active heart rate measurements, in addition to demographic information, including gender and age, to estimate activity-related caloric expenditure.
Active heart rateThe number of times the heart beats per minute during physical activity.

---

### Heart Rate Variability

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual Coaching Lesson 2Biometric EducationHeart Rate Variability Heart rate variability (HRV) is the measurement of variation in time intervals between heartbeats, most commonly reported in milliseconds (ms). Wearable devices are frequently limited to time-domain measurements of HRV and report values as the standard deviation of N-N intervals (SDNN) or root mean squared standard deviation (RMSSD). Additional measures of HRV include nonlinear or frequency-domain analysis, which is beyond the scope of this discussion.
Time-Domain Measurements of HRVIndex
Units
Description
SDNN
ms
Total variability between heartbeats, considered the gold standard when measured over a 24-hour period to predict morbidity and mortality (Task Force of the European Society of Cardiology and the North American Society of Pacing and Electrophysiology, 1996)
RMSSD
ms
Beat-to-beat variance in heart rate to estimate parasympathetic influences (Shaffer et al., 2014)
HRV provides quantifiable and non-invasive insight into the current state of a client’s autonomic nervous system, which is involved in the regulation of autonomic or involuntary processes. The autonomic nervous system consists of two primary branches, sympathetic (fight-or-flight) and parasympathetic (rest, digest, and relax). HRV is a measurement of the ongoing contention between these two branches to regulate physiological processes without conscious thought. For example, an individual does not have to actively think about breathing, their heart beating, or maintaining blood pressure due to autonomic control of these processes. More importantly, though, recent research has shown that changes in HRV may be related to inflammatory processes, hormone regulation, and immune system function (Liu et al., 2017).
VectorMine/Shutterstock.comHeart rate variability (HRV)The measurement of variation in time intervals between heartbeats, most commonly reported in milliseconds (ms).
Autonomic nervous systemThe division of the peripheral nervous system responsible for regulation of involuntary processes that do not require conscious control, such as heart rate or respiratory rate.

---

### Content

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual Coaching Lesson 2Biometric EducationCritical!Heart Rate Variability and Health

Due to its relation to physical and mental health outcomes, HRV is among the most meaningful biometrics that fitness professionals should consider when developing and measuring the success of a lifestyle intervention program. There is growing evidence that various diseases are accompanied by reduced HRV values including, but not limited to, the following (Young & Benton, 2018):


    Diabetes
    Cardiovascular disease
    Psychiatric disorders
    All-cause mortality


In a meta-analysis, researchers found that individuals with lower HRV values compared to their cohort had a 112% higher risk of all-cause death and a 46% increased incidence of cardiovascular events (Fang et al., 2019).
Heart rate variability is a highly individualized biometric and should not be utilized to compare different clients but, instead, assessed for positive or negative change at the individual level. While HRV has been shown to decrease predictably with age (Jandackova et al., 2016) and various health-related conditions, the value of monitoring HRV in a virtual coaching program is to determine an individual’s recovery, daily training readiness, and monitor progress over time. In general, HRV values should be relatively stable from day to day but may increase gradually over time with positive lifestyle interventions, including improved sleep, exercise activity, and nutritional habits. Higher HRV values have been positively associated with improved cardiovascular fitness and stress resilience (Dong, 2016) and decreased risk of training-related injuries (Williams et al., 2017). Additional studies have shown that decreased heart rate variability is an effective tool for measuring cumulative physical fatigue in overtrained athletes (Pichot et al., 2000), which may offer insight into programming periods of lower intensity to prioritize recovery (Figure: Heart Rate Variability).
FigureHeart Rate VariabilityCoach’s CornerGuiding Training With HRV 

HRV represents the current state of the individual’s autonomic nervous system and its ability to make a positive adaptation. By assessing an individual’s HRV trend and adjusting daily training efforts accordingly, fitness professionals can help clients train smarter while also improving overall outcomes.

Training Prescription Guided by HRV in Cycling (Javaloyes et al. 2018)

In a study of elite cyclists, researchers divided participants into two groups: an HRV-guided training group and a traditional periodization group. The HRV-guided training group had their daily output dynamically adjusted based on daily HRV values, whereas the traditional periodization group followed a structured protocol. Following an 8-week training intervention, researchers found that HRV-guided training led to greater increases in the following:


    Peak power (5%)
    Power at second ventilatory threshold (VT2) (14%)
    Power over a 40-minute time trial (7%)

---

### Oxygen Saturation

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual Coaching Lesson 2Biometric EducationOxygen Saturation When an individual breathes, oxygen is attached to and distributed throughout the body by a protein molecule called hemoglobin in the red blood cells. Oxygen saturation, often referred to as SpO2 and reported as a percentage (%) value, is the fraction of oxygenated hemoglobin relative to the total amount of hemoglobin. The normal range for oxygen saturation in healthy adults is 95 to 100 percent at rest (Figure: Normal Oxygen Saturation) but can be as low as 88 to 92 percent in clients with known conditions such as Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease or other lung diseases (Holland, 2019). Lifestyle habits such as smoking may also reduce oxygen saturation (Ozdal et al., 2017).
FigureNormal Oxygen SaturationMonitoring oxygen saturation is important because low blood oxygen (hypoxemia) can lead to adverse effects on important organ systems, including the brain, heart, and kidneys (Hafen & Sharma, 2020). In general, clients with normal oxygen saturation should not recognize significant day-to-day changes or overtime with program interventions. If a significant decrease in oxygen saturation is recognized, particularly if it is accompanied by symptoms, fitness professionals should urge clients to seek medical attention.
HemoglobinA red protein responsible for transporting oxygen in the blood of vertebrates.
Oxygen saturationOften referred to as SpO2 and reported as a percentage (%) value, the fraction of oxygenated hemoglobin relative to the total amount of hemoglobin.

---

### Respiratory Rate

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual Coaching Lesson 2Biometric EducationRespiratory RateRespiratory rate, measured in breaths per minute (bpm), is the number of breaths an individual takes in 1 minute at rest. Respiration is measured as one complete inhale and exhale, which represents the cycle of respiratory gas exchange of oxygen and carbon dioxide. Normal ranges for respiratory rates vary throughout the life cycle (Figure: Respiratory Rates by Age), with newborns breathing at a rate of up to 60 breaths per minute but leveling out at a steady rate of 12 to 20 breaths per minute in adulthood (Nall, 2017). For adults, respiratory rates below 12 or over 25 are considered abnormal and may be indicative of an underlying condition such as asthma, anxiety, pneumonia, heart, or lung disease (Cleveland Clinic, 2019). Respiratory rate may also be influenced by posture or mechanical restrictions of the diaphragm (Zafar et al., 2018).
FigureRespiratory Rates by AgeBased on physical activity and other stressors, respiratory rate can vary significantly throughout the day. Fitness professionals should focus on nocturnal measurements of the respiratory rate due to the relatively controlled environment. In general, the nocturnal respiratory rate should be steady from day to day with minimal change over time. Minor changes in nocturnal respiratory rate may be indicative of recent alcohol consumption (Izumi et al., 2005), whereas rapid and significant changes may be indicative of illness requiring medical attention (Cretikos et al., 2008).
Coach’s CornerAssessing Respiratory Rate During Activity 

In response to physical activity, the autonomic nervous system will increase the respiratory rate to account for increased oxygen demand and a change in blood pH due to the increased concentration of carbon dioxide. Changes in respiratory rate measured during physical activity have been shown to be a valid and reliable marker for assessing cardiorespiratory fitness and anaerobic threshold during a graded exercise test (Carey et al., 2005), which may be valuable for program development. Unfortunately, most wearable devices are intolerant to motion and do not provide a reliable measurement of respiratory rate during physical activity.
Respiratory rateThe number of breaths an individual takes in 1 minute at rest, typically measured in breaths per minute (bpm).

---

### Sleep Parameters

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual Coaching Lesson 2Biometric EducationSleep Parameterssolar22/Shutterstock.comAdequate, quality sleep is an important yet overlooked aspect of health and physical performance. According to the Centers for Disease Control (Colton & Altevogt, 2006), one-third of adults in the United States have insufficient sleep. This correlates to wide-ranging effects on the cardiovascular, endocrine, immune, and nervous systems, leading to an increased risk of health conditions, including the following (Center for Disease Control, 2020):


    Obesity
    Type II diabetes
    Hypertension
    Cardiovascular disease
    Anxiety and depression
    Decreased testosterone

General sleep recommendations will vary by age group but may not be appropriate for all client populations depending on health status and level of physical activity. Fitness professionals should monitor the client’s sleep duration in relation to other biometric parameters, including sleep quality and heart rate variability to determine more precise sleep recommendations within the appropriate guidelines (Figure: Sleep Recommendations).
FigureSleep Recommendations

---

### Content

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual Coaching Lesson 2Biometric EducationGetting TechnicalSleep, Recovery, and Adaptation 

While sleep seems like a time to finally let the brain “shut off,” research has revealed the brain works even harder during sleep. During sleep, the brain works to restore and repair cells, process information for long-term memory, and perform important processes related to health and performance (Dattilo et al., 2011).

Fitness professionals should emphasize the importance of proper sleep for recovery and dynamically modify programs to optimize client outcomes and decrease the risk of injury.
Wearable devices provide fitness professionals with the ability to consistently monitor clients’ sleep habits to determine deficiencies and develop data-driven programs for improvement. Wearable devices auto-capture meaningful sleep parameters and often provide an overall sleep score for rapid interpretation of change over time.
Andrey_Popov/Shutterstock.comCommon Wearable Device Sleep ParametersSleep Parameter
Description
Time in Bed
Total time spent in bed, which may include time spent watching television or on waking before getting prepared for the day
Sleep Duration
Total time spent asleep – sleep duration relative to the time in bed can be utilized to assess sleep efficiency
Onset and Wake Time
Time of sleep onset and wake up time – these values can be utilized to assess consistency in sleep schedule or adherence to a circadian rhythm
Sleep Stages
Analysis of time or percentage of time spent in each distinct sleep stage – sleep stages vary by wearable device but often include light sleep, deep sleep, and REM sleep
Latency
The amount of time to fall asleep
Awakenings/Disruptions
The number of awakenings, which may include voluntary awakenings such as using the restroom or involuntary, including a sleep disruption

---

### Content

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual Coaching Lesson 2Biometric EducationAdditional valuable sleep parameters provided by certain wearable devices include movement, snoring levels, circadian rhythm scores, sleep accounting, and overall sleep scores ranging from 0 to 100 for simple interpretation. Each reported sleep metric provides a fitness professional with the opportunity to develop an individualized and data-driven program to improve sleep quality for overall program success.
FigureOverview of Sleep StagesGetting TechnicalIntroduction to Sleep Stages

There are four distinct stages of sleep each with the purpose of restoring or improving specific physiological processes. In general, the body will cycle through all of these stages several times throughout a single night, but various lifestyle factors, ages, and health conditions can impact the amount of time spent in each sleep stage.

FIGURE: Overview of Sleep Stages
Critical!Circadian Rhythm 

One of the most meaningful, simple, and cost-effective (free) interventions that a fitness professional can recommend is adherence to a consistent sleep cycle. The human body has evolved to follow a natural and internal 24-hour clock, or circadian rhythm, which impacts body temperature, metabolism, and hormonal processes (National Institute of General Medical Sciences, 2020). Physiological processes are optimized when factors such as sleep and wake up times are consistently maintained. Consistency in sleep patterns has been correlated with improved cognitive function and memory, whereas inconsistent circadian patterns have shown to increase the incidence of illness, cardiovascular disease, and cancer (Scheiermann et al., 2013). The human body’s natural circadian rhythm can be disrupted by external factors, including temperature and light.

Fitness professionals may consider circadian rhythm interventions or recommendations to maintain consistent sleep onset and wake up times to improve an individual’s overall sleep quality and performance.

---

### Recovery and Readiness

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual Coaching Lesson 2Biometric EducationRecovery and ReadinessRecovery and readiness to engage in physical activity are relative concepts that require access to consistent and reliable biometric data. For example, a fitness professional cannot compare two individuals side-by-side and determine who is more recovered based on a heart rate variability value. Instead, it requires an understanding of an individual’s baseline values over the previous days or months to determine the current state of their body. Regarding the common biometrics provided by wearable devices, fitness professionals should assess recovery and readiness based on the biometrics that have been clinically validated to predict the risk of injury, daily readiness to perform, or change in health status. These biometrics are resting heart rate, heart rate variability, and sleep parameters (Figure: Assessing Recovery and Readiness).
Parilov/Shutterstock.comFigureAssessing Recovery and Readiness

---

In the example provided in Table: Example of Inadequate Recovery – RECOVER, the client did not achieve adequate sleep and biometrics have not fully recovered, indicating a potential increased risk of injury or poor performance (Williams et al., 2017; Buchheit et al., 2009). The fitness professional should consider modifying the program to focus on active or passive recovery and recommend that the client get a restful night's sleep.
For the purposes and simplicity of these examples, sleep duration was utilized as a primary biometric to assess recovery and readiness. Note that sleep duration alone should not be relied on to determine sleep quality and readiness. Additional parameters, including sleep stages and disruptions, should be included in the determination of sleep quality.
Following a defined baseline stage, many wearable applications apply unique algorithms to provide a recovery score ranging from 0 to 100 for rapid interpretation and simple understanding. These often include basic instructions to rest and recover, take it easy, or engage in light physical activity or indicates readiness to engage in rigorous physical activity based on full recovery.
Recovery Template

### Building a Program

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual CoachingLesson 3Building a ProgramBuilding a ProgramThe value of integrating wearable technology into virtual coaching is not from the data collection itself but the insight this data provides to the fitness professional for individualized and data-driven program development. When used properly, wearable technology and the data it provides can be utilized to develop programs and make data-driven adjustments that optimize outcomes and safety. Wearable technology data can also provide a behavioral reinforcement tool for ongoing motivation, adherence, and retention.
Milles Studio/Shutterstock.com

---

### Process Overview

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual CoachingLesson 3Building a ProgramProcess OverviewTo optimize the efficiency and value of integrating wearable technology into virtual coaching, the fitness professional should develop a consistent and repeatable process (Figure: Program Design).
FigureProgram DesignIntegrating Wearables Into Virtual CoachingPhase
Overview/Description
1 – Baseline
Establish a minimum of a 3- to 5-day baseline to obtain objective insight into a client’s biometric strengths and deficiencies. During the baseline phase, the client should be instructed to continue with normal lifestyle behaviors and not adjust nutritional or exercise habits.
2 – Analysis and Initial Program Development
Following the baseline phase, fitness professionals should perform a comprehensive analysis of a client’s strengths and deficiencies in relation to their goals. Programs should be developed to address deficiencies that may limit program success while ensuring that strengths are not sacrificed. A comprehensive program is developed and provided to the client with an explanation of WHY specific interventions were chosen.
3 – Ongoing Monitoring
Fitness professionals should develop a consistent schedule for monitoring client data. While it may not be feasible to assess data every day, it is important to check in on days that rigorous training is scheduled to assess readiness and, if necessary, adjust program recommendations. Some wearable platforms provide automated alerts of significant change to streamline the monitoring process.
4 – Adjust & Consult
Utilize biometric data to dynamically adjust program recommendations based on client recovery and readiness to safely and effectively perform the recommended program for the day. Over time, fitness professionals may be able to create a more effective program schedule based on recovery trends from previous exercise interventions.

If a program adjustment is required, fitness professionals should attempt to understand the unexpected change in biometric data. For example, poor sleep quality and decreased HRV may be explained if the client consumed alcohol the evening prior. This represents an opportunity to show the client how this behavior impacted their biometrics to create awareness and avoid future deviations from the program.

In the case that positive biometric trends are recognized, this represents an opportunity to provide positive reinforcement to maintain program adherence.

---

### Intervention Toolkit

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual CoachingLesson 3Building a ProgramIntervention ToolkitFitness professionals should have a comprehensive understanding of the biometrics provided by wearable technologies and develop an intervention toolkit based on their skills and qualifications. This may be organized based on which biometric it is validated to address and then selected on a client-by-client basis based on analysis of additional assessments, cost, and time commitment. Over time, the toolkit will be enhanced and refined based on personal experiences and program outcomes.
Coach’s CornerProgram Development – Practical Example

Client A is a 45-year-old male with a desk job and a high-level of work-related stress. After a check-up from his doctor, Client A was diagnosed as pre-diabetic and obese. Client A’s physician cleared him for exercise but emphasized the importance of lifestyle changes. Client A has tried to exercise and improve his nutritional habits but gives up because the scale weight does not improve.

Following a comprehensive assessment and establishing a 5-day biometric baseline, the fitness professional identified the following deficiencies:


    2400 steps/day
    Sleep latency over 30 minutes
    Lack of consistency in sleep times
    Elevated resting heart rate (88 bpm)
    Reduced HRV (28 ms)


Based on the fitness professional’s qualifications, it is determined that a minimally invasive and phase-based program will be the most effective and sustainable for Client A:


    Daily step count goal of 5000 steps, increased by 500 steps per week: To emphasize consistency of movement throughout the day, Client A is instructed to set an alarm and take at least 500 steps every hour during the workday.
    Exercise program (based on other assessments)
    Nutritional counseling (based on nutritional assessment forms)
    Sleep recommendations and Circadian Rhythm Intervention: Avoid use of technology for 1 hour prior to bed, create a bedtime routine, and set consistent sleep and wake times each day.
    Education: Why these biometrics are important, expected improvements, and the significance to Client A’s health and goals


The fitness professional will monitor Client A’s biometric data for compliance and consistently provide positive reinforcement based on improvements in sleep quality, daily physical activity, and other applicable biometrics.

---

### Data-Driven Adjustments

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual CoachingLesson 3Building a ProgramData-Driven AdjustmentsHuman physiology is complex and adaptable. This requires fitness professionals to consistently monitor and dynamically adjust programs to minimize the risk of training-related accidents, avoid injuries, and optimize client outcomes. The use of wearable technology provides fitness professionals with the unprecedented ability to objectively quantify and track the efficacy of any type or combination of interventions. For example, a fitness professional with the proper qualifications may develop a similar nutrition program for two individuals who present with similar backgrounds and biometrics but recognize the high probability of significantly different health and body composition outcomes.
goodluz/Shutterstock.comThe following are examples of various data-driven adjustments:


    Adjusting the type or intensity of a single exercise session based on recovery data
    Adjusting a portion of an intervention to test if outcomes can be improved, such as modifying macronutrient recommendations
    Removing an intervention that does not seem to be yielding outcomes
    Removing an intervention that is negatively affecting biometric data
    Adding an intervention based on changes in biometric data over time
    Dynamic modification of a program to avoid plateaus


For example, a previously sedentary client following an exercise intervention may require additional sleep to facilitate adequate recovery.

---

### Behavioral Considerations

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual CoachingLesson 3Building a ProgramBehavioral ConsiderationsThe basic principles of human behavior state that behaviors that are reinforced tend to be maintained. Clients have likely attempted lifestyle modification on their own prior to seeking help from a fitness professional but failed because they did not recognize improvements and could not maintain intrinsic motivation. Utilizing biometric data from wearable devices provides fitness professionals with the ability to consistently monitor compliance, which is a notable limitation of virtual coaching, and provides a source of positive reinforcement to optimize ongoing momentum for improvement (Figure: Reinforcement Theory of Motivation). Fitness professionals should actively pursue any opportunity to make a client feel good about their progress, whether it be based on compliance to a program or positive biometric trends related to overall health and performance (Expert Program Management, 2018).
FigureReinforcement Theory of MotivationAdditionally, biometric data can be utilized to bring attention to the negative impact of certain behaviors. For example, a decrease in sleep quality and heart rate variability following a night of heavy alcohol consumption may decrease the likelihood of the individual deviating from the program in the future.

Lastly, clients want to know that they are being supported and monitored by a fitness professional they can trust. By integrating wearable technology into virtual coaching, fitness professionals have the ability to further individualize recommendations and continuity of care with 24/7 monitoring beyond what can be offered in a training facility.
Positive reinforcementA stimulus following a behavior that increases the likelihood of an individual continuing that behavior – For example, improvements in scale weight due to nutritional intervention increase the likelihood of an individual continuing to adhere to the nutrition program.

---

### Summary

Chapter 7Integrating Wearables Into Virtual CoachingLesson 3Building a ProgramSummaryBiometric data provided by wearable technologies provides fitness professionals with reliable data to assist in developing, modifying, and continuously monitoring virtual programs for client compliance and outcomes. Many of these biometrics, including sleep and heart rate variability, may be applied to support a better-quality program for all client types based on their relationship with physical health, mental health, risk of injury, and athletic performance. When integrated properly, wearable technology provides the fitness professional with the ability to facilitate a 24/7 monitoring approach to improve client outcomes while also optimizing their ability to scale their virtual coaching business. # Chapter 8: Selling Virtual Coaching

### Chapter 8: Selling Virtual Coaching

Chapter 8Selling Virtual CoachingLesson 1Introduction to Sales, Pricing, and PackagingChapter 8: Selling Virtual CoachingIt may seem counterintuitive, but many companies lose sales because they try to provide solutions for everyone instead of focusing on a smaller, yet profitable market. Understanding individual expertise and personal goals will help fitness professionals develop a niche, unique selling proposition, and pricing model to differentiate from the competition and drive sales.
Learning ObjectivesAfter completing this chapter, the NASM Virtual Coach will have the ability to do the following:


    Identify customer problems to develop sales strategy.
    Summarize how to develop and customize sales goals.
    Describe the sales process.
    Summarize the customer journey process.
    Create a prospecting strategy.

---

### Introduction

Chapter 8Selling Virtual CoachingLesson 1Introduction to Sales, Pricing, and PackagingIntroductionWithout sales, any business will cease to exist. A fitness professional who owns their own business must give careful thought and consideration to the best methods to sell virtual coaching. Sales can be intimidating and can even sometimes have a negative connotation, but it does not have to be this way. Many new business owners assume that sales will come naturally; however, this is rarely the case. Much like helping clients achieve goals, sales require training, systems, processes, and consistent practice to yield results for the business. Sales are an outcome of a repeatable and scalable process. Without a well-developed sales process, sales will likely never come. A virtual fitness professional must develop scalable, teachable, and repeatable processes to help their businesses grow to become a successful entrepreneur (Figure: Fitness Professional Entrepreneur).
FigureFitness Professional EntrepreneurWhen starting a traditional or virtual business, the fitness professional is typically not only a business owner but also a business operator, spending their hours and efforts working both in the business and working on the business. However, being a business owner-operator should not be the end goal, as this model is likely not sustainable. The end goal of the business-owning fitness professional should be to become a true entrepreneur. An entrepreneur does not simply own or operate a business, they run a business. The entrepreneur works diligently to create clear and efficient business and sales processes. They utilize and leverage automation so they can focus on other aspects of their business. They value positive and negative feedback. Entrepreneurs strive for not only improving their business but improving themselves.
This chapter will provide the fitness business entrepreneur with tools necessary to build a successful sales program that will be repeatable, largely automated, and will scale with their business as it grows. When applicable, each section will contain a writing prompt with at least three questions to help the entrepreneur develop a reliable sales strategy for their business.
Coach's CornerPlease note: At the end of each section there are three or more writing prompts to help the fitness professional develop their own well-thought-out and detailed sales plan. The sales plan prompts should be completed after reading the entire chapter, as many of these questions and ideas work off each other. To develop a complete picture of what the fitness professional’s sales plan will look like, it is important to understand all of the concepts presented in this chapter first.
Business ownerAn individual or group who owns a business entity and has final decision-making authority as well as first right to profits.
Business operatorThe individual running the day-to-day operations of a business.

---

### Defining Sales

Chapter 8Selling Virtual CoachingLesson 1Introduction to Sales, Pricing, and PackagingDefining SalesPurchasing is a solution to a problem. When consumers have an issue or unmet need, they typically purchase a product or service they feel will solve that specific problem or meet that specific need. As soon as the transaction is complete, the company this product or service was purchased from has made a sale. Selling a product or service is problem solving. Consequently, sales are the process of truthfully and successfully conveying that a company’s product or service offering truly solves the consumer’s problem.
Fitness business entrepreneurs take on many different duties, especially when starting and growing their business. One of the most important tasks a virtual fitness business entrepreneur will have is that of a salesperson. As previously mentioned, without sales, a company will cease to exist. Many business owners, and people in general, have an aversion to sales. Salespeople are sometimes viewed as manipulative and willing to do whatever it takes to make a sale—even at the customer’s expense. The stereotypical salesperson shows little regard for the interest of the customer and cares more about getting money in their pocket. In contrast, the fitness business entrepreneur is not a stereotypical salesperson and will not be viewed as such, provided they have a long-term mindset about their business and emphasize putting their customers’ interests first. Fitness business entrepreneurs generally are passionate about the product or service they offer and believe it will make a significant impact on their client’s life. By putting ethics and clients’ interests first, there is no need to worry about coming across as a manipulative or unethical salesperson. While it probably does not need to be stated, manipulation and poor ethics do not produce results in the long run.
As many retail and financial transactions now take place via e-commerce web and mobile applications, prospective clients will also likely expect a virtual coaching business to conduct its sales online. Leveraging the familiarity that potential clients already have with e-commerce, automated sales processes can be developed by the fitness professional that will turn their business into a smooth-running sales machine.
Inbound and Outbound SalesSales can be divided into two categories: inbound sales and outbound sales. Outbound sales are sales initiated by the business—in other words, the business contacted the client first to attempt a sale. Outbound sales are associated with techniques such as cold calling, where the business contacts prospective clients unsolicited.
Antonov Maxim/Shutterstock.comConversely, inbound sales are sales initiated by the client—in other words, the client contacted the business looking to purchase a particular product or service.

The virtual fitness professional’s customer acquisition strategy should be built around an inbound sales approach. Inbound prospective clients include those who have found the business via content-driven platforms like social media channels, blogs, videos, podcasts, etc. Inbound strategies are centered on establishing trust and building rapport with prospective customers. Successful inbound methods help establish the brand as a trusted advisor through high-quality content. By earning prospective clients’ trust and interest they will be more receptive to sales tools the fitness professional entrepreneur will utilize to generate business, such as automated e-mail marketing. With that in mind, this chapter will focus primarily on inbound sales processes.
ConsumerIndividuals in the target audience who are not yet aware of the business or its products and services.
Outbound salesSales that are initiated by the business; the business contacted the consumer first.
Inbound salesSales that are initiated by the consumer; the consumer contacted the business first.

---

### Why People Make Purchases

Chapter 8Selling Virtual CoachingLesson 1Introduction to Sales, Pricing, and PackagingWhy People Make PurchasesConsumers purchase products or services to solve a problem. When clients and potential clients are not satisfied with their current health and fitness or are not attaining their desired fitness and health goals, they typically seek out goods and services to help fulfill that desire. For the virtual fitness professional, most of their clients will have similar goals, including weight loss, building muscle, obtaining better overall health, improving performance, etc. The fitness professional’s mission is to identify the problems their clients are facing, inform the client how their programs and services can help solve their problem, and ethically guide the future client in their purchase of the business’s services (Figure: Fitness Professional’s Mission).
FigureFitness Professional's MissionIdentify the ProblemUtilizing the target market identified in their business plan, the fitness professional has a well-defined market audience. Correctly identifying the specific problems clients are seeking to solve is key to developing a successful sales funnel. Note that a client’s problem or problems may change over time. It is always important to obtain feedback on how the goods and services are meeting the needs of the target market, not only from clients but also from potential clients who do not currently purchase the service.
When starting a business, the fitness professional does not always have enough, or any, feedback from their potential market. To solve this, the fitness professional must conduct market research to understand the most common challenges prospective clients are facing. At this stage, the fitness professional may not have clients or even potential clients whom they can contact for useful feedback, but they can still develop an idea based on their own knowledge and experience about what problems and challenges the market needs to solve and overcome. The focus of the initial sales strategy should be the problems that currently exist in the market that inspired the fitness professional to develop their own virtual fitness business.
Sales Strategy Questions for Identifying the ProblemA useful exercise is to ask a series of questions to progressively narrow down the focus and help identify the primary problem future programs and services will solve.


    What are three problems that everyone, not just the target customer, faces that makes them seek out the expertise and guidance of a fitness professional?
    What are two problems (same as above or otherwise) that virtual coaching solves for clients compared to traditional in-person training?
    What is one problem that is unique to your target market that you feel you can solve better than anyone else?

Sales funnelAn organized, repeatable process that a prospective customer follows to become a paid customer.

---

### Solve the Problem

Chapter 8Selling Virtual CoachingLesson 1Introduction to Sales, Pricing, and PackagingSolve the ProblemWith the problem identified, and the fitness professional already having established that they can solve the problem with their service, the next step is to understand how to successfully communicate the problem and solution to a potential client. Advertising and marketing are the keys to conveying this message.
HstrongART/Shutterstock.comMarketing and sales are closely related, and advertisements are an integral part of sales. Advertisements typically convey solutions to identified problems. Consider television ads for soft drinks. They largely focus on people relaxing, quenching their thirst, and cooling off with an ice-cold drink. The problem is thirst and the solution is the soft drink. If an ad for a soft drink stated, “You’re going to be thirsty sometime, and when you are, buy our drink,” the advertisement would not have much impact. However, if the advertisement clearly demonstrates how the product can fix a problem, the impact is much greater. When attempting to convey that a product solves a problem, the fitness professional should be mindful to not only highlight the obvious solution but also focus on the reasons why the problem exists in the first place.
Sales Strategy Questions for Solving the Problem
    What are three reasons, or subproblems, why your target market’s problem exists?
    What is a solution for each of the three subproblems identified?
    Construct a paragraph of sales copy that conveys the overall problem, subproblems, and solution.

Sales copyWriting with the specific goal of guiding a consumer through a sale.

---

### Emotional Factors in Purchasing

Chapter 8Selling Virtual CoachingLesson 1Introduction to Sales, Pricing, and PackagingEmotional Factors in PurchasingConsumers buy products and services for a variety of reasons. The Consumer Emotional Intelligence Scale (Kidwell et al., 2008) was designed to investigate the role that emotional factors play in consumer’s purchasing decisions. The three emotional factors examined include self-concept connection, emotional attachment, and brand love. This study found focusing on emotional connections with consumers, rather than traditional features and benefits, resulted in greater brand loyalty. For the fitness entrepreneur, this should be the main focus of the sales strategy. When attempting to build an emotional connection with customers, authenticity is essential, with 81% of consumers stating they must be able to trust a brand (Edelman, 2019).
With a solid understanding of the problems their programs and services solve, the virtual fitness professional can put themselves in their clients’ and prospective clients’ shoes and imagine the emotions that they may feel about the problems they are experiencing. Selling based on emotions is not an attempt to take advantage of the negative emotions people feel due to a certain problem but to highlight the positive emotions a person will feel when they realize the fitness entrepreneur’s product solves the problem.
Sales Plan Questions for Emotional Factors in Purchasing
    What is one negative emotion a consumer feels for each of the subproblems identified in the previous section?
    What are two possible positive emotions for each subproblem once solved?
    How does the client feel prior to utilizing the service? What positive emotions will be revealed once the services are delivered? (Write a detailed paragraph.)

---

### Prices and Packages

Chapter 8Selling Virtual CoachingLesson 1Introduction to Sales, Pricing, and PackagingPrices and PackagesWhen pricing a core product, a fitness professional must ensure their pricing aligns with the target market being served. A very low-priced service being offered to high-income earners will have a difficult time finding clients who value the service. A service whose target market is seniors on a fixed income may not find clients if the service is priced too high. The pricing level of a product should not be confused with the quality or value of a product. A product can be economically priced but of high quality and value, and conversely, a luxury priced product can be of very poor quality and not offer much value.
Pricing StrategiesThe business plan should define the target market and provide a pricing analysis of competitors in the market. The virtual fitness professional should set the base price of the service in the range of market analysis. Once the base price of a product is set, the fitness professional can then use different pricing strategies to aid in the sales process. The four pricing strategies discussed in Chapter 2: Building a Business include economy, skimming, penetration, and premium pricing. Additionally, the fitness professional may also consider using promotional pricing and bundle pricing strategies (Figure: Pricing Strategies).

Penetration pricing is used to build interest in a new product. With this method, the standard price of the product is initially reduced as an introductory offering. However, this pricing strategy may result in a price so low the business cannot sustain selling at this rate for long periods of time. When penetration pricing is used, a clear objective, or cut-off, must be noted. Penetration pricing objectives may include initially obtaining a specific number of clients, converting those clients to higher-priced programs or services, increasing client referrals, or having a specific and publicly announced end date for the promotion.
FigurePricing StrategiesVectorpocket/Shutterstock.comPromotional pricing is similar to penetration pricing but with a few distinct differences. First, promotional pricing is offered to an existing product or service. Second, the discount should not be as low as a penetration pricing discount. With an existing and established product, offering a very large discount runs the risk of upsetting existing customers, along with devaluing the services that are provided. If a product or service is regularly on sale, most customers will wait until promotional pricing is offered again, rather than purchase at the standard price.

Bundle pricing is a strategy that offers discounts for buying in bulk. This can either be buying one product in bulk or purchasing multiple products together for a discounted price. For example, different bundle discounts could be offered on monthly coaching if a client pre-pays for 3, 6, or 12 months of coaching when compared to paying monthly. A bundle discount for clients who purchase multiple products would cover clients who purchased fitness training and nutrition coaching together instead of purchasing fitness training or nutrition coaching alone.

### Training Packages

Chapter 8Selling Virtual CoachingLesson 1Introduction to Sales, Pricing, and PackagingTraining PackagesThe fitness professional does not need to have a one-size-fits-all approach to their packages. While a price point for the business is set in the business plan (economy, midgrade, or luxury), programs and services offered can still fall within a range of those price points. Providing different options to target the different needs of clients will allow a business to offer more personalized solutions to clients’ specific needs and desires.
For each product developed, the entrepreneur can easily create and establish three price levels for that program or service, with each price level offering more or fewer features.

### Sales Goals

Chapter 8Selling Virtual CoachingLesson 2Sales Goals and Process CustomizationSales GoalsAs with most areas in life, goals are critical to creating a strong sales strategy. There are many different methods to set and track sales goals. This section will examine three different approaches to setting and tracking sales goals and provide reasonable recommendations for ensuring that goals are attainable and achieved. These approaches are defining sales goals, tracking sales, and prospecting clients.
Defining Sales GoalsOverall, a sales goal is an established revenue target or objective generated by program and service sales. Sales goals can be broken down into a virtually infinite number of subcategories to fit any business structure. However, the two most basic subcategories for defining sales goals are timeframe and sales stage. Sales goals can be broken down and tracked in three simple timeframes, including daily, weekly, and monthly.
FigureSales GoalsThree basic sales stages to be tracked include new prospects, leads, and customers (Figure: Sales Goals). These goals represent the three different stages a customer enters during the sales cycle and will be expanded on later in this chapter. Using the timeline and sales stage method, the fitness professional now has three simple goals to track (Table: Sales Goals Tracking).
Sales Goals TrackingSales Goals

Monthly
Prospects

Customers
ProspectA person whom the business has identified as fitting their client criteria but has not yet provided their information.
LeadsProspects who have shown interest in a company and have provided identifying information, such as an email address.
CustomersLeads who have purchased a product or service from a company; may also be used interchangeably with the term clients.

### Content

Chapter 8Selling Virtual CoachingLesson 2Sales Goals and Process CustomizationSales goals should be adjusted, at a minimum, on a quarterly basis to ensure the target numbers are not only realistic but are bringing in enough new clients to fulfill the fitness professional’s vision for the business and financial need. The fitness professional can define the goals and timeframes that work best for their business; however, in this chapter, the above sales goals will be referenced and utilized for working examples.
DGLimages/Shutterstock.comThe sales goal tracking process can also be used for existing clients. For example, a fitness professional may create a goal to contact new clients after 30 days to see if they are interested in upgrading their virtual coaching package from training only to both nutrition and training. While these are existing clients in their respective categories of virtual coaching, they are leads for upgrading to a higher-priced service. It is always easier to sell to an existing client than it is to sell to a new client. Existing clients are already familiar with the business and less time and effort is needed to build trust and establish rapport compared to a potential customer.
Table: Sample Virtual Fitness Sales Goals illustrates how a simple sales goal process can potentially generate large amounts of consistent, predictable revenue for a business.

Before the fitness professional has historical data to use for creating sales goals, all projections are educated guesses. For this example, it is assumed that 200 people per day will become prospects of the business. These prospects come from viewing the business’s website, blogs, social media posts, etc. The example assumes that 5% of these prospects will turn into leads. The conversion from prospects to leads will change over time as leads do not go away after only 1 week. For example, in e-mail marketing, a lead may stay on a company's mailing list for months or years before becoming a customer. From these leads, the assumption is made that 5% will convert to paid customers every month. Again, this number will change over time. It is the fitness professional’s job to gradually improve the percentages of prospects, leads, and customer conversions. Incremental improvements add up over time.
This example used simple numbers and educated guesses for conversion rates. Sales and conversion goals must be updated regularly to align with financial need and provide the entrepreneur with the most realistic targets possible.

### Tracking Sales

Chapter 8Selling Virtual CoachingLesson 2Sales Goals and Process CustomizationTracking SalesWith sales goals established, the fitness professional must continually track their sales data to ensure realistic goals have been set. Using the metrics of daily, weekly, and monthly prospects, leads, and customers, the fitness entrepreneur has a powerful dataset for tracking sales and revenue. Each metric will have unique data points and places to source this data. To obtain sales data, the source of each metric being tracked must be clearly defined.
Each business should have personalized definitions of prospects, leads, and customers, but, in general, they will be similar to the following parameters for tracking. Prospects include unique views on the company website, comments on social media, podcast downloads, and video views. Prospects should be tracked daily. Leads include inbound e-mails and contact form submissions from the company website, direct messages on social media, inbound phone calls, downloading free ebooks in exchange for the lead’s email address, and receiving any contact information from other means. Lead data will be tracked daily but recorded in the sales tracker weekly. Customers include anyone who has made their first purchase from the company or a client who returns after previously ending their services. For sales goals tracking, this number generally does not include those on recurring subscriptions. Customer data will be tracked daily but only recorded in the sales goal tracker monthly.
Eviart/Shutterstock.comEach sales stage category should be updated weekly with the previous week’s data. This allows the fitness entrepreneur to track and stay on top of sales metrics and identify if they start to trend down, stay consistent, or trend upward. While nothing may need to be modified, it allows for visibility and tracking of revenue goals, and the fitness professional can determine how they may average out over time.
Helpful HintJust as declining sales are something the entrepreneur should be on alert for, explosive sales are also something to be aware of. Growth is vital for a business, but explosive growth is not sustainable for most small businesses and can have several negative consequences.

### Prospecting for New Clients

Chapter 8Selling Virtual CoachingLesson 2Sales Goals and Process CustomizationProspecting for New Clients Prospecting is the act of searching for new clients. Prospects turn into leads and leads turn into clients. Finding prospects can often be a challenging and intimidating aspect of sales for small business owners, typically with outbound sales or outbound prospecting. The method of cold-calling prospects from a purchased sales list, such as telemarketing, is typically no longer used. There are many ways to find prospects for leads and, ultimately, new clients. This section will focus on how to utilize the business’s target audience, as defined in the business plan, and marketing plan to automate the prospecting process.
FigureCommon Sales TypesThere are two types of sales: business to consumer sales (B2C) and business to business sales (B2B) (Figure: Common Sales Types). Fitness professionals who target the B2B market might be focused on services such as corporate wellness programs. In B2B marketing, the target customer is a business. Most virtual fitness professional’s sales will fall into the B2C category, in which the target customer is an individual consumer. This section will focus primarily on the B2C category.
Prospecting, just like sales and marketing, can be broken down into inbound prospecting and outbound prospecting. Inbound prospecting builds on the marketing processes developed and will allow the prospecting process to become largely automated. An inbound prospect finds the company’s content through various channels and content types defined in the marketing plan. These channels and content can include social media, search engines, pay-per-click (PPC) advertising, email marketing, videos, infographics, newsletters, etc. The goal is to convert the prospect who viewed or consumed the marketing content into a lead, and that lead in turn will provide the business with their contact information. This process will be discussed in greater detail later in this chapter. Outbound prospecting is when the target customer audience is first identified by the business and then, the business reaches out to the target customer and initiates contact. Outbound prospecting is an effective prospecting technique, but it is time-consuming and limits the ability to automate the prospecting process.
Prospecting must be continually performed, as businesses generally always need new customers. Prospecting can be developed and simplified into a repeatable and scalable process by focusing on three areas (Figure: Prospecting Process). First, clearly define the target market. Second, identify a clear prospecting strategy of how and where prospects will be found. Finally, establish a clear new prospecting strategy to ensure new prospects continue to become aware of the company.
FigureProspecting ProcessProspectingIdentifying potential customers.
Business to consumer sales (B2C)Sales from a business directly to a person.
Business to business sales (B2B)Sales from a business directly to another business.
Inbound prospectingThe process of a consumer discovering a business from passive content released by the business.
Outbound prospectingThe process of a business contacting a consumer via direct marketing.

---

### Define Target Market

Chapter 8Selling Virtual CoachingLesson 2Sales Goals and Process CustomizationDefine Target MarketThe first step in automating the prospecting process is to ensure the target market, or target audience, is well-defined. This should be clearly outlined in the business plan, as the target market is used for many different areas of the business, including but not limited to sales and marketing. When defining or refining the target market for use in sales prospecting, the virtual fitness professional should ask three questions. Please note: This is not an all-inclusive list of questions, but it is a great starting point.
Sentavio/Shutterstock.comSales Strategy Questions for Define Target Market
    What is the ideal client’s goal?
    How old is the ideal client?
    How long is the average client enrolled in coaching?


Building on these three questions, the fitness professional can then focus their prospecting efforts on a narrower demographic.

---

### Prospecting Strategy

Chapter 8Selling Virtual CoachingLesson 2Sales Goals and Process CustomizationProspecting StrategyThe second step in automating the prospecting process is developing a strategy for how the business will find new prospects. An example outbound prospecting strategy would be to purchase a list of consumers who fit the target audience and then, either call or e-mail them. This is a time consuming and expensive process, which typically yields poor results.
Utilizing an inbound prospecting approach, the virtual fitness entrepreneur can take advantage of the marketing plan and capitalize on existing content to attract prospective clients. For example, utilizing blog posts that have followed a strategic search engine optimization (SEO) approach, as defined by the marketing strategy, will bring in consumers who are in the business’s target audience directly to the company’s website. These prospects are very high quality because they qualified themselves into the target audience by searching for a question the business can, and already has, answered in their blog post content.
Sales Strategy Questions for Prospecting StrategyThe fitness professional can define how the business will obtain new prospects by answering three questions.


    Based on the marketing strategy, what are the top three content types that will be utilized by the business to attract prospects (videos, infographics, blogs, podcasts, etc.)?
    For each of the content marketing types identified, what incentive will be created and offered for the consumer to share their contact information with the business and become a lead?
    For each incentive, how will the fitness business entrepreneur follow up with the newly created lead? How can this process be automated?

Coach's CornerThe prospecting strategy must focus on refining the content marketing strategy to ensure content developed is directed toward the target audience.

For example, if a fitness professional's target audience is older adults, they may write blog posts about protein intake for seniors. The prospecting strategy should ensure that the blog is optimized for commonly used terms the prospects will type into a search engine.

If the business is utilizing videos in their marketing, the prospecting strategy should ensure the videos offer an incentive for prospects to provide their contact information and become leads.
A well-rounded business needs to have prospects and, consequently, sales coming from more than one place. Having multiple sales pipelines will ensure the business has enough diversity and a strong foundation of prospects that can withstand market changes over time.

---

### New Prospects Strategy

Chapter 8Selling Virtual CoachingLesson 2Sales Goals and Process CustomizationNew Prospects StrategyThe third step in developing and automating the prospecting process is reading a new prospecting strategy. This ensures there is a constant flow of new prospects into the sales pipeline. While content creation is an integral part of the marketing strategy, it is equally, if not more important, for the prospecting strategy. To ensure a steady stream of prospects, content must be created on a regular and consistent basis and focus on topics the target audience is actively seeking knowledge on. For example, a blog post that covers how to design home workouts may appeal perfectly to the target audience. However, if the blog content is three years old and does not consider the latest home fitness equipment, the previous impact the blog may have had is lost and fewer leads will be generated from the outdated content. On the other hand, publishing and promoting monthly blog posts reviewing the latest home exercise equipment will produce new and different prospects regularly.
Pixel-Shot/Shutterstock.comSales Plan Questions for New Prospects Strategy
    Referring to the three types of content marketing identified previously, how often will content be created for each type of content?
    How often will incentives offered to the prospect be updated?
    How can prospects, leads, and clients be incentivized to bring new prospects into the business? (List at least one idea for each.)


Now that the entrepreneur has a well-defined prospecting strategy, they can start building a repeatable and scalable process that can be automated using e-mail marketing and content creation and building a referral network from potential, existing, and former clients.
Getting TechnicalMarketing automation is essential to a growing business. Consider searching for and reviewing articles for additional information about various marketing automation tools on the market.

23 Marketing Automation Tools You Need to Use (Patel, 2020)

The Best Email Marketing Software for 2020 (McLaughlin & Sevilla, 2020)
